9 
II. 
Property Tax Expense 
10 
Q. 
How is the taxable value of the Companies’ real property determined 
11 
for property tax purposes? 
12 
A. 
Property owned by KEDNY and KEDLI located in the public right-of-way 
13 
is considered special franchise property and subject to a special franchise 
14 
assessment.  The preparation of special franchise assessments is the 
15 
responsibility of the Valuation Services Division of the Office of Real 
16 
Property Tax Services (“ORPTS”) with the State Board of Real Property 
17 
Services having the authority to approve assessments.  Real property 
18 
owned by the Companies located on private property is locally assessed.  
19 
For purposes of property tax assessment, real property includes land, 
20 
buildings, plant and other equipment. 
21 
13


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 12 of 88 
 
Regardless whether the property is subject to a special franchise or local 
1 
assessment, the methodology for determining the assessment or taxable 
2 
value of most utility property is the Reproduction Cost New Less 
3 
Depreciation (“RCNLD”) approach to valuation.  
4 
  
5 
Q. 
What was the amount of property tax expense incurred by the 
6 
Companies in the Historic Test Year? 
7 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, Page 1, KEDNY and 
8 
KEDLI’s property tax expense in the Historic Test Year totaled $134.144 
9 
million and $139.628 million, respectively.  
10 
 
11 
Q. 
What is the forecast of property tax expense for the Rate Year and 
12 
Data Years? 
13 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit__ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, Page 2, KEDNY forecasts 
14 
property tax expense of $141.504 million, $153.004 million, and $166.986 
15 
million in the Rate Year, Data Year 1 and Data Year 2, respectively.  As 
16 
shown in Exhibit__ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, Page 2, KEDLI’s forecast of 
17 
property tax expense for the Rate Year and Data Years 1 and 2 is 
18 
$141.613 million, $151.472 million, and $161.824 million, respectively. 
19 
 
20 
14


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 13 of 88 
 
Q. 
How do the Rate Year forecasts compare to the current allowance in 
1 
rates for property tax expense? 
2 
A. 
KEDNY’s forecast of $141.504 million for property tax expense for the 
3 
Rate Year is below the current rate allowance of $145.507 million for 
4 
property tax expense.  KEDLI’s forecast of $141.613 million for property 
5 
tax expense for the Rate Year is higher than the current rate allowance of 
6 
$118.646 million. 
7 
 
8 
Q. 
How was the forecast of property tax expense calculated? 
9 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI began with the calendar year (“CY”) 2015 property 
10 
tax expense, which was normalized to add back property tax refunds of 
11 
$0.38 million for KEDNY and $1.118 million for KEDLI received during 
12 
the year and reverse any deferrals that were recorded.  The normalized CY 
13 
2015 expense was adjusted for known changes in the obsolescence 
14 
allowance to be applied to the tax roll.  The Companies then applied a 
15 
growth factor of 0 percent for KEDNY and 5.22 percent for KEDLI for 
16 
the bridge period of CY 2016 to reflect forecast increases in property tax 
17 
of $0.0 million for KEDNY and $7.1 million for KEDLI, respectively.  
18 
(Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, Page 3).  The Companies added to these 
19 
amounts forecast property tax expense associated with incremental 
20 
additions to plant in service of $3.1 million for KEDNY and $0.9 million 
21 
15


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 14 of 88 
 
for KEDLI for CY 2016.  This process was then repeated for the Rate 
1 
Year and Data Years.  (Exhibit __ (RRP-5), Schedule 1, Page 3). 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please explain how the Companies determined the proposed growth 
4 
factor. 
5 
A.   
KEDNY proposes a growth factor of 0 percent based on an adjusted one-
6 
year experienced growth rate from CY 2014 to CY 2015.  KEDNY’s 
7 
experienced growth rate for this period was -9.16 percent; however, this 
8 
was due in large part to an unusual 11.24 percent decrease in the Class 3 
9 
(Utility Class) share of the New York City total property tax levy, which 
10 
KEDNY believes is anomalous and will not repeat in subsequent years.  
11 
Historically, New York City has had a lower cap on the residential class 
12 
(Class 1 and 2), thereby shifting tax liability to Class 3.  However, the cap 
13 
was not lowered for CY 2015.  The published taxable value data and tax 
14 
rates for CY 2016 show no further decrease in the Class 3 share of the tax 
15 
levy for CY 2016.  In light of the impact of this shifting of tax levy and 
16 
changes in obsolescence (discussed below), KEDNY believes a growth 
17 
factor of 0 percent is appropriate.  As discussed below, the 0 percent 
18 
growth factor for KEDNY is supported by the results of the back-cast.  
19 
 
20 
16


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 15 of 88 
 
 
KEDLI proposes a 5.22 percent growth rate using the one-year 
1 
experienced growth rate from CY 2014 to CY 2015.  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please explain how tax rates are established. 
4 
A. 
Tax rates are set by local taxing jurisdictions (school district, municipality, 
5 
county, special district).  Each jurisdiction determines its revenue from all 
6 
sources other than property taxes such as state aid, sales tax revenue, and 
7 
user fees.  Those revenues are subtracted from the jurisdiction’s budget 
8 
and the remainder becomes the property tax levy.  To determine the actual 
9 
tax rate, the taxing jurisdiction divides the tax levy by the total taxable 
10 
assessed value of all property in the jurisdiction. 
11 
 
12 
Q. 
How did the Companies calculate the average tax rate for the Historic 
13 
Test Year? 
14 
A.   
The Companies calculated their average tax rate for the Historic Test Year 
15 
in three steps. 
16 
1. The Companies identified all tax parcels for which the Companies 
17 
paid property taxes, including the location of the parcel, its 
18 
assessed value, the equalization rate of the municipality where 
19 
each parcel is located, and the taxes paid.  
20 
17


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 16 of 88 
 
2. The Companies then divided the assessed value of each parcel by 
1 
the applicable equalization rate for the municipality, as established 
2 
by ORPTS, to calculate 100 percent of taxable value for each 
3 
property. 
4 
3. Finally, the Companies divided the total property taxes paid by the 
5 
result of Step 2 above. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
How do the Companies propose to reflect changes in the tax rate for 
8 
the Rate Year and Data Years? 
9 
A.   
Changes in tax rates for the Rate Year and Data Years are implicit in the 0 
10 
percent and 5.22 percent annual growth factors used by KEDNY and 
11 
KEDLI, respectively.  For purposes of determining the property taxes 
12 
associated with the incremental plant additions, the Historic Test Year’s 
13 
average tax rate is used.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
Does the property tax cap law in New York State affect the forecast?  
16 
A. 
No.  In 2011, New York State passed a law capping year-on-year growth 
17 
on the property tax levy at two percent or the rate of inflation, whichever 
18 
is less.  This law does not constrain the Companies’ forecast of property 
19 
tax expense because the law does not cap increases in tax rates or 
20 
individual tax bills.  The Companies’ forecast of property tax expense is 
21 
18


--- Page 21 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 17 of 88 
 
based on tax bills, not the tax levy.  Additionally, the cap on tax levy is not 
1 
an absolute limit.  There are exceptions for certain expenses (e.g., 
2 
unusually large pension increases) and, with a sixty percent majority, 
3 
voters for school districts or local governments can vote to override the 
4 
cap.   
5 
  
6 
Q. 
How do the Companies manage property tax expense? 
7 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI manage property tax expense by protesting 
8 
overvaluations, reviewing cost data supplied to taxing authorities, and 
9 
petitioning for obsolescence allowances to be applied to the valuation of 
10 
special franchise property.  KEDNY and KEDLI are also working with 
11 
Staff and ORPTS to mitigate property tax expense associated with the 
12 
Companies’ accelerated leak prone pipe replacement programs 
13 
prospectively.  
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What is obsolescence and how does it affect property taxes? 
16 
A. 
Obsolescence is an impairment in the value of an asset that is distinct from 
17 
physical depreciation.  The RCNLD methodology used by ORPTS to 
18 
develop assessments recognizes the existence of functional and economic 
19 
obsolescence.  To date, the economic obsolescence allowed by ORPTS is 
20 
based on the Companies not achieving certain economic performance 
21 
19


--- Page 22 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 18 of 88 
 
metrics.  If these metrics improve, the amount of economic obsolescence 
1 
allowed by ORPTS will decrease, causing a corresponding increase in 
2 
assessments and property taxes.   
3 
 
4 
Q. 
Has ORPTS accepted the Companies’ applications for obsolescence 
5 
allowances? 
6 
A. 
Yes, ORPTS has accepted the Companies’ applications for obsolescence 
7 
as shown in the table below. 
8 
Table 1 – Obsolescence Allowances 
9

10 
Q. 
Please explain the Companies’ forecast obsolescence rate for the Rate 
11 
Year and Data Years. 
12 
A. 
The Companies forecast obsolescence for the Rate Year using the 
13 
percentage allowed by ORPTS for the 2016 assessment roll of 15.8 
14 
percent and 32.0 percent for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively.  The Data 
15 
Years were forecast using the change in the allowances for 2015, which 
16

17

Q. 
What is the impact of capital additions on the forecast?  
4 
A. 
Capital additions increase the Companies’ taxable property and their 
5 
ultimate property tax expense.  KEDNY’s 0 percent and KEDLI’s 5.22 
6

7

8

9

10 
the forecast property tax expense.  
11

14 
A. 
As discussed, refunds were added back to the Historic Test Year.  Refunds 
15 
are the result of successful litigation against municipalities and are 
16 
therefore non-recurring and unpredictable.  KEDNY and KEDLI do not 
17 
have a basis for forecasting refunds that may be received in the Rate Year 
18 
or Data Years.

Q.

1

3

4

5

9

10

11

12 
added the calculated additional tax on net plant closings in excess of, or 
13 
under, the average change to net plant in service using the same three-year 
14 
period used in preparing the forecast for the Rate Year and Data Years.

19

Q. 
Are the Companies proposing a reconciliation mechanism for 
4 
property tax expense? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  KEDNY and KEDLI currently defer 90 percent of the difference 
6 
between their actual property and special franchise tax expense and the 
7 
amount reflected in rates.  The Companies are proposing to continue this 
8 
mechanism.  Given the variability, uncertainty and potential difficulties 
9 
associated with forecasting property tax expense, the Companies believe 
10 
that continuing the current reconciliation mechanism is both reasonable 
11 
and appropriate.  Economic instability that could impact assessments and 
12 
tax levies, difficulty in forecasting the impact of a new rate plan on 
13 
obsolescence, and other factors outside the Companies’ control (e.g., New 
14 
York City shifting tax levy among classes) all make forecasting property 
15 
tax expense for KEDNY and KEDLI particularly difficult.

III. 
Uncollectible Expense and Credit and Collections Processes 
18 
Q. 
Please describe KEDNY and KEDLI’s recent experience with 
19 
uncollectible expense. 
20 
23


--- Page 26 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 22 of 88 
 
A. 
KEDNY’s uncollectible expense increased from $16.5 million in 2013 to 
1 
$18.7 million in the twelve months prior to the Historic Test Year, and 
2 
then decreased to $17.9 million during the Historic Test Year.  KEDLI’s 
3 
uncollectible expense increased from $6.6 million in 2013 to $17.9 million 
4 
in 2014, and then decreased to $10.3 million during the Historic Test 
5 
Year.  These figures are presented in Exhibit __ (SSP-4). 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Please describe how KEDNY and KEDLI manage their collections 
8 
processes to minimize uncollectible expense. 
9 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI use a full suite of collection activities and strategies 
10 
to manage their collection processes and minimize uncollectible expense.  
11 
These activities and strategies are employed throughout the Companies’ 
12 
delinquency management process and consist of four key components: 
13 
Account Initiation, Account Management, Field Collections and Final Bill 
14 
Management. 
15 
 
16 
Account Initiation is the point at which the Companies establish a service 
17 
contract with the customer applying for service.  Prior to initiating service, 
18 
the Companies confirm the identity of each applicant.  If an applicant has 
19 
outstanding debt with the Companies within the last six years, he or she is 
20 
required to pay the balance owed or establish a payment agreement prior 
21 
24


--- Page 27 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 23 of 88 
 
to obtaining service.  The debt is recalled from the collection agency, if 
1 
applicable, before a payment agreement is established for the unpaid 
2 
balance.  The past debt is then transferred to the new active account under 
3 
the terms of the payment agreement.    
4 
 
5 
Account Management is the method the Companies use to actively 
6 
monitor customer payment history.  The Companies manage collections 
7 
accounts with the use of a behavioral scoring mechanism referred to as a 
8 
Portfolio Management Package (“PMP”).  Each customer account is 
9 
evaluated and scored using PMP.  The output from the model, which is 
10 
reviewed and updated at regular intervals, assists the Companies in 
11 
determining the appropriate collections actions based on the customer’s 
12 
past payment behavior.  Customers are divided into five risk groups and 
13 
each group is assigned the most cost-effective approach likely to be 
14 
successful.  In prioritizing the accounts in the portfolio, KEDNY and 
15 
KEDLI seek to identify lower risk customers that will likely pay and 
16 
higher risk customers requiring more assertive collection measures.  This 
17 
process attempts to tailor the response to the specific circumstances of the 
18 
individual customer and ensure that the most cost-effective steps are taken 
19 
to mitigate the Companies’ uncollectible expense.  Additionally, the 
20 
Companies have increased the use of targeted outbound calling campaigns 
21 
25


--- Page 28 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 24 of 88 
 
to improve effectiveness in encouraging customers to take action 
1 
regarding their arrears.   
2 
 
3 
Field collections begin after a final notice has been issued to the customer.  
4 
Field collections are the actions taken by the Companies to collect 
5 
payments in a manner that is mindful of customer rights, protections and 
6 
safety.  As a last resort, service may be terminated because of non-
7 
payment.   
8 
 
9 
Final Bill Management is the process of collecting final or written off 
10 
accounts with the assistance of outside collection agencies.  Since 
11 
converting to the Customer Service System (“CSS”) in 2013, KEDLI has 
12 
utilized a tool that enables it to manage this collections workstream and 
13 
track and execute strategies to maximize resource performance and 
14 
minimize costs. 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
How have the collections processes affected uncollectible expense? 
17 
A. 
Uncollectible expense incurred by KEDLI and KEDNY each year is a 
18 
significant focus of the Companies’ attention, as are efforts to mitigate this 
19 
cost.  In response to upward pressure in uncollectible expense, the 
20 
Companies implemented a bad debt mitigation plan in 2008, which 
21 
26


--- Page 29 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 25 of 88 
 
continued through the Historic Test Year.  As part of the bad debt 
1 
mitigation plan, certain collection activities were ramped up, particularly 
2 
in the areas of outbound calling, account initiation, and field visit 
3 
management. 
4 
   
5 
The Companies’ ability to mitigate bad debt has been challenged during 
6 
the last five years by events and conditions.  These include: (a) the 
7 
devastating impact of Superstorm Sandy and its aftermath on customers; 
8 
(b) record-breaking cold winters in 2013-14 and 2014-15, which drove up 
9 
heating usage and left customers with much higher than average bills to 
10 
manage; (c) reduced federal heating assistance for low income customers 
11 
that translated to a lower share of their increased heating costs being 
12 
covered; (d) an increased number of protected customers in the KEDNY 
13 
and KEDLI service territories, together with customers who benefit from 
14 
provisions requiring the Companies to offer $10 minimum payment 
15 
agreements; (e) summer/winter temperature thresholds for service 
16 
terminations; and (f) voluntary caps on terminations.   
17 
 
 
 
18 
Q. 
Are KEDNY and KEDLI taking any other steps to control their 
19 
uncollectible expenses? 
20 
27


--- Page 30 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 26 of 88 
 
A. 
Yes.  Starting in 2014, KEDNY undertook a program to reinstate 
1 
previously written-off accounts and transfer the unpaid balances to active 
2 
accounts (“Transfer-to-Active Program”).  The Transfer-to-Active 
3 
Program matches customers with active service or those seeking new 
4 
service with previously written off amounts in the customer’s name.  For 
5 
KEDNY, unpaid balances of $7.336 million were transferred to active 
6 
accounts from June 2014 through September 2015.  While KEDNY’s 
7 
Transfer-to-Active program is largely manual, KEDLI leverages its 
8 
customer system to transfer unpaid balances on an ongoing basis to 
9 
current active accounts with the advantage of fuller automation.  While the 
10 
manual process used by KEDNY is not as efficient as KEDLI’s more 
11 
automated process, it has been effective in collecting approximately 34 
12 
percent of KEDNY’s transferred balances through September 2015.  
13 
    
14 
During implementation, KEDNY’s Transfer-to-Active Program resulted in 
15 
a significant short term reduction in net write-offs, as written off balances 
16 
were transferred back to accounts receivable.  Following this initial 
17 
reduction in net write-offs, many of the transferred balances remain 
18 
unpaid, which results in a period of higher write-offs as the program 
19 
progresses.  Recognizing that the transfers result in an artificially lower 
20 
uncollectible rate during early stages of implementation, KEDNY 
21 
28


--- Page 31 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 27 of 88 
 
proposes to normalize the uncollectible expense data to remove the effect 
1 
of the Transfer-to-Active Program on the uncollectible rate.  To normalize 
2 
the data over the proposed three-year period used to determine the 
3 
uncollectible rate, KEDNY added the $7.336 million back into net write-
4 
offs for June 2014 through September 2015, and simultaneously removed 
5 
the original write-off from the period in which it occurred.  This 
6 
normalization has the effect of treating the resulting write-offs during the 
7 
three-year period as if the Transfer-to-Active Program had never occurred. 
8 
 
9 
The following table illustrates the uncollectible rate by month with two 
10 
lines.  The “With Normalized Data” line illustrates the rate with the active 
11 
dollars normalized.  The “Without Data Normalized” line illustrates the 
12 
rate without the active dollars normalized.  As shown in the chart, 
13 
initiation of the Transfer-to-Active Program resulted in an artificially 
14 
lower uncollectible rate as a result of the short term reduction in net write-
15 
offs.   
16 
 
17 
 
 
 
 
29

Table 2– Transfer-to-Active Program

Q. 
Please discuss the Companies’ efforts to assist low income customers 
4 
during the collections process.

A. 
The Companies strive to inform low income customers of programs 
6

7

8

10

11

12

13

14 
30

Testimony of Shared Services Panel

to assist low income customers in managing their arrears, which, in turn, 
1

2 
 
3 
Q.  
Please describe how uncollectible expense is currently recovered by 
4 
KEDNY and KEDLI. 
5 
A. 
Commodity-related uncollectible expense is recovered through the Gas 
6 
Adjustment Clause (“GAC”) and determined by multiplying the forecast 
7 
total gas costs for each GAC year times a net write-off rate of 1.48 percent 
8 
for KEDNY and 0.75 percent for KEDLI.  The commodity-related 
9 
uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI is adjusted for actual gas 
10 
costs annually in the normal course of the GAC reconciliation.  Delivery-
11 
related uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI is recovered 
12 
through base delivery rates and is not adjusted.     
13

5 
Q.  
Why are KEDNY and KEDLI proposing an uncollectible rate based 
6 
on the three-year aggregated average? 
7 
A. 
The Companies’ proposal to use a three-year aggregated average mitigates 
8 
the impacts of significant external events such as Superstorm Sandy, the 
9 
Long Island Power Authority’s (“LIPA”) transfer to a new system 
10 
manager, the CSS conversion, and the extreme cold weather experienced 
11 
in the winters of 2013-2014 and 2014-2015.  For example, because of the 
12 
unusual cold temperatures, there were only 22 out of 81 days available to 
13 
disconnect service for non-payment during the winter of 2014-2015.   
14 
Additionally, to respond to the increased number of leaks resulting from 
15 
the unusually cold temperatures, KEDNY redirected field resources from 
16 
field collections to leak investigations.  A three-year average helps 
17 
mitigate the impact of these weather-related conditions.  The three-year 
18 
aggregated average also mitigates the impact of customer protections, 
19 
which were at their highest level in the Historic Test Year, resulting in 
20 
33


--- Page 36 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 32 of 88 
 
lower write-offs than if the Companies had the ability to terminate such 
1 
customers. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
What is the forecast uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI for 
4 
the Rate Year and Data Years? 
5 
A. 
Using the three-year aggregated average uncollectible rates of 1.0571 
6 
percent for KEDNY and 1.0593 percent for KEDLI, and multiplying that 
7 
rate by each Company’s respective forecast retail revenues (including late 
8 
payment charge revenue and excluding 18-A assessments and power 
9 
generation revenues), the uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI for 
10 
the Rate Year is $13.864 million and $9.042 million, respectively.  The 
11 
uncollectible expense for KEDNY and KEDLI for Data Years 1 and 2 is 
12 
shown in Exhibit __ (RRP-3), Schedule 31.  The calculation of 
13 
commodity-related uncollectible expense, which is embedded in the total 
14 
uncollectible expense of $13.864 million and $9.042 million for KEDNY 
15 
and KEDLI, respectively, is shown in Exhibit __ (RDP-6). 
16 
 
17 
Q.  
How is commodity-related uncollectible expense currently 
18 
determined?  
19 
A. 
A charge for commodity-related uncollectible expense is recovered from 
20 
firm sales customers.  This charge is developed for KEDNY and KEDLI 
21 
34


--- Page 37 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 33 of 88 
 
prior to the start of each Gas Cost Year from an estimate of total gas cost 
1 
recoveries.  This estimated gas cost recovery is then multiplied by the 
2 
uncollectible expense fixed experience factors set in KEDNY and 
3 
KEDLI’s last base rate cases (Cases 06-G-1185 and 06-G-1186).  A per 
4 
dekatherm charge applicable to firm sales customers is then developed for 
5 
each Company by unitizing this expense by projected firm sales.  At the 
6

7 
recoveries to the estimate and implements an annual imbalance factor to 
8 
credit over recoveries or surcharge under recoveries of commodity-related 
9 
uncollectible expense.  
10

Q. 
Do the Companies propose to change the calculation of commodity-

related uncollectible expense for firm sales customers?

A. 
Yes.  The Companies propose to implement an approach that is aligned 
14 
with the method currently utilized by NMPC.  The Companies are 
15 
proposing a calculation of commodity related uncollectible expense for 
16 
firm sales customers that is updated each month coinciding with changes 
17 
to the monthly cost of gas filed in the monthly GAC.  With this approach, 
18 
there is no need to perform an annual reconciliation because the monthly 
19 
commodity-related uncollectible expense charge is aligned with the

monthly cost of gas.  The testimony of the Rate Design Panels provides

2 
 
3 
Q. 
Do the Companies have any other proposals relating to credit and 
4 
collections? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies are proposing to create a performance incentive 
6 
measure based on the rate of service terminations.  This proposed 
7 
performance measure is intended to provide an incentive for the 
8 
Companies to limit the number of terminations and to address the 
9 
Commission’s interest in utilities pursuing alternatives to terminations.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Has the Commission established metrics relating to service 
12 
terminations in other proceedings? 
13 
A. 
In recent rate cases and the ongoing Reforming the Energy Vision 
14 
(“REV”) proceeding (Case 14-M-0101), the Commission has encouraged 
15 
utilities to pursue alternatives to service terminations of residential 
16 
customers while also mitigating bad debt write-offs.  In setting service 
17 
termination incentive thresholds, the Commission has followed a 
18 
methodology similar to that used for establishing service quality metric 
19 
performance thresholds, which entails reviewing a five-year average 
20 
36


--- Page 39 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 35 of 88 
 
experience of terminations and the standard deviation in terminations over 
1 
that period.   
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Would this approach for establishing thresholds for service 
4 
terminations be appropriate for KEDNY and KEDLI? 
5 
A. 
No.  The Companies submit that a threshold based on the number of 
6 
terminations for the most recent five year periods for KEDNY and KEDLI 
7 
would not adequately reflect the successive downward pressures on 
8 
terminations experienced by the Companies.  This downward pressure was 
9 
particularly acute for KEDNY in 2015, when field collections resources 
10 
were redirected to work on leak investigations and nearly 20,000 fewer 
11 
terminations were performed than was typical for prior years.  For this 
12 
reason, KEDNY proposes to exclude 2015, and use a five year average for 
13 
the period 2010 through 2014.  Because KEDLI’s terminations have been 
14 
strongly impacted by external factors, KEDLI proposes to use the 
15 
termination rate for 2015 as a threshold.  In particular, the LIPA separation 
16 
had a pronounced effect on KEDLI’s termination rates, as KEDLI was 
17 
formerly able to rely upon electric terminations to avoid gas terminations 
18 
for combined gas/electric customers.  With the separation of the electric 
19 
service, KEDLI must now rely solely on gas terminations to respond to 
20 
non-payment.  Superstorm Sandy and the temporary suspension of 
21 
37


--- Page 40 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 36 of 88 
 
collections operations following the CSS conversion also support use of 
1 
the 2015 termination rate for KEDLI. 
2 
 
3 
The above approach is necessary to develop reasonable thresholds for 
4 
KEDNY and KEDLI.  In comparison to other large utilities in New York, 
5 
both KEDNY and KEDLI have tended to terminate fewer residential 
6 
customers as a percentage of those with accounts in 60 day arrears over 
7 
the last five years.  On average, the nine major NYS utilities terminated 35 
8 
percent of such accounts for non-payment.  In comparison, during the 
9 
same period, KEDNY and KEDLI terminated only 26 percent and 14 
10 
percent of such accounts, respectively.  Indeed, KEDLI is an outlier 
11 
among New York utilities, with the lowest percentage of terminations over 
12 
the past five years.   
13

IV. 
Customer Service Quality Programs 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ efforts to manage customer service 
6 
quality. 
7 
A. 
The centerpiece of the Companies’ customer service efforts are the 
8 
customer call centers in MetroTech Center in Brooklyn, New York.  The 
9 
MetroTech location houses two separate call centers, one for KEDNY and 
10 
one for KEDLI.  
11 
 
12 
Both call centers are full service centers providing 24 hours a day, 365 
13 
days a year emergency service.  The call centers handled over 3.5 million 
14 
calls combined in 2014.  The customer call center staff includes 150 
15 
KEDNY and 90 KEDLI professional customer service representatives 
16

17

A.  Existing Service Quality Metrics 
10 
i. Percent of Calls Answered in 30 Seconds

12

A.  
This metric measures the percent of calls answered by a live customer 
14 
agent in less than thirty seconds.  Calls handled and abandoned in the 
15 
IVR system are not part of the calculation.  
16 
 
17 
  
Currently, this metric is included in only KEDNY’s service quality 
18 
program.  KEDNY’s performance target was updated from 52.9 percent 
19 
to 59.0 percent in 2013.  Notwithstanding, KEDNY is proposing to again 
20 
update the target to a more stringent 62.2 percent.  To align the 
21 
46


--- Page 49 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 45 of 88 
 
Companies’ service quality programs, KEDLI is proposing to include 
1 
this metric in its program and to use the same 62.2 percent target used for 
2 
KEDNY.  The proposed targets assume the adoption of the Companies’ 
3 
proposals, discussed below, to add incremental call center staffing and to 
4 
load balance calls.  To the extent these proposals are not adopted, 
5 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s targets would need to be modified.  
6 
 
 
 
7 
ii. Adjusted Customer Bills  
8 
Q.  
What is the definition for the proposed adjusted customer bills metric 
9 
and how is it calculated? 
10 
A.  
The adjusted customer bills metric is a measure of the proportion of 
11 
customer bills that require later adjustment because of errors by the 
12 
company, expressed as a percentage of total customer bills issued by the 
13 
company.  The metric is measured monthly and performance is based on 
14 
the average of the twelve monthly scores.  KEDNY and KEDLI’s current 
15 
target is 1.69 percent.   
16 
 
17 
The draft report in the Data Audit recommended that, in KEDNY’s next 
18 
rate case, KEDNY and the Commission reach agreement on what 
19 
constitutes an adjustment due to an error by the company, and that once 
20 
an agreement is reached, KEDNY’s target be reset.    
21 
47


--- Page 50 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 46 of 88 
 
During this past summer, an adjusted bills working group met pursuant to 
1 
recommendations in the Data Audit.  Although the definition of an 
2 
adjusted bill was not finalized, the group discussed that the following 
3 
situations would not constitute an adjustment due to company error (and 
4 
thus should be excluded from the metric’s calculation): 
5 
• 
an estimated bill replaced by a bill based on an actual reading 
6 
• 
a customer reading replaced with an actual or estimated 
7 
reading 
8 
• 
a change in a customer bill as a result of switching to or from 
9 
budget billing and 
10 
• 
changes to correct for the responsible billing party (e.g., 
11 
where bills have been rendered to a prior resident, and bills 
12 
are re-issued to the current resident) 
13 
The Companies believe that these exclusions are appropriate and have 
14 
used them to calculate more stringent adjusted bills targets.  The new 
15 
targets are 0.58 percent for KEDNY and 1.24 percent for KEDLI. 
16 
 
17

10 
B.  New Service Quality Metric 
11 
i.  Payment Processing 
12 
Q.  
What is the definition for the proposed payment processing metric 
13 
and how is it calculated? 
14 
A.  
Payment processing is a new metric that measures exception payments.  
15 
An exception payment is a payment that cannot be processed because of 
16 
an error caused by either the customer (e.g., sending a check without an 
17 
account number) or the Companies.  This results in a delay in posting 
18 
payment to the customer’s account.  The payment processing metric 
19 
measures exception payments caused by company error.   
20 
 
21 
50


--- Page 53 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 49 of 88 
 
There are two categories of errors caused by the Companies – item 
1 
processing errors and procedural errors.  An example of an item 
2 
processing error is where the customer included the correct account 
3 
number on the check, but the Companies entered the wrong account 
4 
number into the system.  An example of a procedural error is where the 
5 
Companies processed a non-negotiable item as a check.  
6 
 
7 
The metric is a measure of the proportion of total exceptions due to 
8 
company error, per 100,000 payments processed.  For purposes of the 
9 
metric, errors made by the Companies’ vendor performing check 
10 
payment processing are considered errors by the company.   
11 
Because there were only eleven months of data available to determine 
12 
thresholds for the payment processing metric, the Companies will 
13 
continue to gather data and present targets in Corrections and Updates. 
14 
 
15 
ii.  IVR Self-Service Rate 
16 
Q.  
Please explain the proposed metric for IVR self-service rate and how 
17 
it is calculated. 
18 
A.  
This is a new metric measuring the percent of customers using the IVR 
19 
that are able to complete their task through the automated system.  
20 
KEDNY and KEDLI customers can complete a number of transactions 
21 
51


--- Page 54 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 50 of 88 
 
via self-service options in the IVR such as making a payment or 
1 
reporting a meter reading.   
2 
  
3 
The metric is based on the percentage of customers that call into the IVR, 
4 
and are able to complete a transaction regarding their account (e.g., make 
5 
a payment, report a meter read) without speaking to a call center 
6 
representative.  
7 
 
8 
iii.  Appointments Kept 
9 
Q.  
What is the definition for the percent of appointments kept metric 
10 
and how is it calculated? 
11 
A.  
The appointments kept metric is based on an existing performance 
12 
indicator, but uses a revised definition, reflecting the input of the 
13 
working group in the ongoing Data Audit proceeding.  The working 
14 
group discussed defining an appointment as any scheduled meeting 
15 
between the company and the customer (or customer’s representative) at 
16 
the customer’s premises in which a date and time or timeframe is agreed 
17 
upon by both parties.  The Companies propose to utilize this definition. 
18 
 
19 
In addition, the Companies propose that the metric apply to appointments 
20 
made and performed by the Companies’ Customer Meter Services 
21 
52


--- Page 55 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 51 of 88 
 
(“CMS”) organization.  Appointments made and performed by CMS 
1 
constitute more than 95 percent of appointments performed by KEDNY 
2 
and KEDLI employees.  Examples of appointments that will be included 
3 
in the metric are meter reads, turn-ons, turn-offs, and meter changes.  
4 
Under the proposed metric, an appointment is considered kept if the 
5 
Companies satisfy the terms of the appointment (date, time, and 
6 
location).  In addition, an appointment will be considered kept if the 
7 
customer is unavailable at the premises when the Companies arrive 
8 
within the scheduled appointment window, or where the customer is 
9 
available at the premises before the scheduled window and the customer 
10 
consents to the earlier time.  
11 
 
12 
iv.  Web/Mobile Payments

Q.  
What is the definition for the proposed percent of payments made

This is a new incentive metric measuring payments received through One 
16 
Time Payment on the Companies’ website, mobile website or mobile 
17 
application, as a percentage of total payments received.  For CY 2015, 
18 
percent of payments made through web/mobile was 8.1 percent for 
19 
KEDNY and 9.7 percent for KEDLI.  Incentive-only targets for percent 
20 
payments made through web/mobile have been set at 20 percent above 
21 
53


--- Page 56 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 52 of 88 
 
CY 2015 performance (9.7 percent for KEDNY and 11.6 percent for 
1 
KEDLI).

i.  Low Income Outreach and Assistance Programs 
4 
Q.  
What is the definition for the proposed low income outreach and 
5

This is a new incentive metric measuring the number of low income 
7 
program participants assisted with a KEDNY or KEDLI billing matter or 
8 
referred to a health and human service agency by a Consumer Advocate 
9

14 
Q. 
Please describe the mechanics of the Companies’ proposed service 
15 
quality programs, including how the negative revenue adjustments, 
16 
offsets and incentives will work. 
17 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit ___ (SSP-9), except for the two incentive only 
18 
metrics, each metric has three targets (a Penalty Threshold, an Offset 
19 
Target and a Penalty Floor Target) along with a penalty amount and an 
20 
offset amount.  
21 
54


--- Page 57 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 53 of 88 
 
Similar to how the current service quality program operates, if the 
1 
Companies miss the Penalty Threshold target for a metric, they are subject 
2 
to a penalty.  Because some of the metrics are based in part on customers’ 
3 
perception of things over which the Companies have no control, such as 
4 
weather or commodity prices, the Companies are proposing to include an 
5 
opportunity to offset underperformance on one metric with superior 
6 
performance on another.  If the Companies achieve the Offset Target – 
7 
which is set at a level designed to achieve stretch performance – the offset 
8 
amount is applied towards any penalty amount.   The Companies cannot, 
9 
however, offset the penalty if performance on the missed metric falls 
10 
below the Penalty Floor Target.   The offsets only apply where a penalty 
11 
has been incurred and therefore do not result in an incentive.  The 
12 
Companies only have an opportunity to earn an incentive for the two 
13 
incentive metrics.   
14 
 
15 
Exhibit __ (SSP-10) provides detailed illustrations of how the Penalty 
16 
Thresholds, Offset Targets and Penalty Floor operate, along with 
17 
examples showing the interaction of offsets and penalties. 
18 
 
19

Are the Companies proposing any revisions to the potential penalties 
20 
associated with customer service performance?

55


--- Page 58 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 54 of 88 
 
A. 
No.  The Companies are not proposing to modify the $11.7 million in total

potential pre-tax penalties for KEDLI. The Companies propose to re-
3

shown in Exhibit __ (SSP-9).

Q. 
Are the Companies proposing any other revisions to how penalties 
7 
associated with customer service performance are handled?  
8 
A. 
Yes.  As part of the National Grid and KeySpan merger, the Commission 
9 
doubled the penalties contained in the Gas Safety, Reliability and 
10 
Customer Service Performance Requirements Joint Proposal (“JP-3”) and 
11 
further ordered that the penalties be tripled in any year in which a dividend 
12 
restriction is triggered, and quadrupled for any year in which a measure is 
13 
not met and had not been met in any two of the prior four years.  The 
14 
Commission’s stated purpose of the doubling, tripling and quadrupling 
15 
provision was to ensure that the merger did not result in an unacceptable 
16 
drop in customer service quality.  Subsequent to the merger, the 
17 
Companies have demonstrated consistent good performance on service 
18 
quality metrics.  KEDNY has met all of its metrics since 2008.   Even 
19 
though KEDLI has missed metrics in recent years, these were due to 
20 
56


--- Page 59 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 55 of 88 
 
factors largely outside of KEDLI’s control such as Superstorm Sandy and 
1 
the extreme cold weather.   
2 
 
3 
The Companies are not proposing to change the current penalty amounts 
4 
that were doubled.  However, the merger-related tripling and quadrupling 
5 
provisions are no longer necessary or appropriate for the Companies’ 
6 
customer service quality programs, and the Companies propose they be 
7 
eliminated.

Call Center and Customer Offices

Q. 
Please describe the Companies’ proposed changes to their call center 
11 
operations. 
12 
A. 
The Companies believe the customer experience could be enhanced by 
13 
leveraging National Grid’s combined teams in New York City and upstate 
14 
New York, along with National Grid’s in-state call center partners.  This 
15 
will permit the Companies to better manage customer interactions by 
16 
dynamically load balancing call volumes throughout the day, transferring 
17 
calls during peak periods among National Grid’s New York call centers 
18 
and third-party partners located in New York.  Flexibility in managing call 
19 
centers would enable the following benefits: 
20 
 more balanced response time 
21 
57


--- Page 60 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 56 of 88 
 
 more consistent service levels 
1 
 ensure skills are maintained for business continuity purposes 
2 
 mitigate risks when storms or mass transit interruptions prevent call 
3 
center personnel from getting into the office and 
4 
 leverage capacity in other centers to allow call center representatives 
5 
additional time for training and coaching. 
6 
 
7 
Therefore, in the interest of enhancing customer service, the 
8 
Companies intend to utilize call centers across National Grid’s New York 
9 
service territory, and hereby provide notice of this proposed change to the 
10 
Commission pursuant to Section 65.13(b) of the Public Service Law.

2 
 
3 
Q. 
What is the status of KEDNY’s plans to upgrade its customer service 
4 
system? 
5 
A. 
KEDNY’s current customer service system is the Customer Related 
6 
Information System (“CRIS”).   While the system adequately supports 
7 
customer billing for KEDNY’s 1.2 million customers, KEDNY has 
8 
contemplated converting from CRIS to CSS.  The planned conversion is 
9 
currently on hold as KEDNY evaluates options.   Because the CRIS 
10 
system was designed to support KEDNY’s specific billing requirements, 
11 
conversion to a new system is a very complicated undertaking.  Therefore, 
12 
KEDNY is evaluating whether CSS or another application would be the 
13 
best choice given the complexities of KEDNY’s customer billing.  The 
14 
hold also gives KEDNY the opportunity to complete its installation of 
15 
AMRs before converting the billing system, thereby avoiding one of the 
16 
challenges with KEDLI’s billing system conversion.  Accordingly, 
17

VII.  Low Income Programs 
1 
Q.    
Please identify the Companies’ low income programs and what 
2 
changes they propose.  
3 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI have demonstrated a longstanding commitment to 
4 
low income programs and continue to work proactively to protect their 
5 
most vulnerable customers.  The Companies understand that low income 
6 
customers in their service territories face substantial challenges in meeting 
7 
their energy needs.  The diversity of demographics, energy usage profiles, 
8 
localized weather, housing, and varying cost of living across the 
9 
Companies’ service territories can all impact energy affordability.  Energy 
10 
costs place a larger burden on low income families and represent a larger 
11 
percentage of their income than other households.  The Companies are 
12 
committed to finding the right balance between providing benefits to those 
13 
customers with the greatest need and program costs.  
14 
 
15 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s low income programs include the Residential 
16 
Reduced Rate and On Track programs.  KEDNY also has a Reconnect Fee 
17 
Waiver Program for customers served under the SC1BR – Residential 
18 
Heating Reduced Rate Service or SC1AR – Residential Non-Heating 
19 
Reduced Rate Service who have had their service disconnected for non-
20 
payment.  This program provides a one-time waiver of the $60 
21 
66


--- Page 69 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 65 of 88 
 
reconnection charge if the reconnection is at the meter or an existing curb 
1 
valve meter, or a $150 reconnect fee if the reconnection requires 
2 
excavation of the street.  Pursuant to its Tariff, KEDLI does not charge 
3 
low income customers reconnect fees.   
4 
 
5 
 The Companies also utilize consumer advocates to reach out and assist 
6 
the most vulnerable customers such as those who are struggling to pay 
7 
their bills and those with special needs.   
8 
 
9 
The Companies propose to revise their Residential Reduced Rate 
10 
programs and to discontinue their On Track programs.  No change is 
11 
proposed to the Reconnect Fee Waiver.  As noted previously, KEDLI also 
12 
proposes to add two consumer advocates to staff its new Brentwood 
13 
Customer Assistance and Outreach Center.   
14 
 
15 
Q. 
What is the On Track program and why do the Companies propose to 
16 
discontinue it? 
17 
A. 
On Track is an arrearage forgiveness/finance management program 
18 
available to single and two family residential gas heating customers with 
19 
arrears greater than $400.  The program has four elements: deferred 
20 
payment agreements, money management education, account management 
21 
67


--- Page 70 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 66 of 88 
 
and arrears forgiveness for payments made on time and the completion of 
1 
the program.  In recent years, the On Track program has not been 
2 
successful.  In 2014, six percent of participants asked to be removed from 
3 
the program, 27 percent completed it and the remaining 67 percent 
4 
defaulted from the program.  Initial review of results for 2015 suggests a 
5 
similar outcome.  Moreover, the program is labor intensive and expensive 
6 
to administer.  Therefore, the Companies propose to eliminate the On 
7 
Track programs and direct the On Track resources to programs that 
8 
provide more benefits to customers.  
9 
 
 
10 
Q. 
Who is eligible to participate in the Companies’ Residential Reduced 
11 
Rate programs? 
12 
A. 
The Companies’ eligibility criteria for their Residential Reduced Rate 
13 
programs target those customers most likely to benefit from them.  
14 
KEDNY and KEDLI customers who have received a Home Energy 
15 
Assistance Program (“HEAP”) grant are automatically enrolled in the 
16 
program when the grant is received.  Customers remain in the program for 
17 
14 months following receipt of the last HEAP grant.  In addition to 
18 
automatic enrollment, the Companies have historically relied on manual 
19 
enrollment to expand the scope of eligibility.  KEDNY and KEDLI utilize 
20 
manual methods such as calls to the call center or referrals from customer 
21 
68


--- Page 71 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 67 of 88 
 
advocates or social services agencies to enroll both heating and non-
1 
heating customers in their Residential Reduced Rate programs.  Manual 
2 
enrollment helps identify a wide range of customers, including customers 
3 
who would qualify for HEAP but have not applied for HEAP benefits.  
4 
The Companies’ existing manual enrollment process identifies 
5 
approximately 10 percent of all participants in KEDLI’s low income 
6 
programs and 33 percent of all participants in KEDNY’s low income 
7 
programs, utilizing the following criteria:  
8 
 Temporary Assistance for Needy Families (Family 
9 
Assistance)  
10 
 Safety Net Assistance - Public Assistance  
11 
 Supplemental Security Income  
12 
 Medicaid  
13 
 Food Stamps  
14 
 Low Income Home Energy Assistance Program 
15 
 Veteran’s Disability Pension  
16 
 Veteran’s Surviving Spouse Pension 
17 
 Child Health Plus  
18 
 
19 
Q. 
Do the Companies propose that non-heating customers be included in 
20 
their low income programs? 
21 
69


--- Page 72 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 68 of 88 
 
A. 
Yes.  The guidance provided in Staff’s Report and Straw Proposal in Case 
1 
14-M-0565 (issued June 1, 2015) (“Straw Proposal”) is that non-heating 
2 
low income customers should not be eligible for benefits.  However, the 
3 
Companies have been providing low income program benefits to eligible 
4 
non-heating customers for years.  In the last major rate proceeding for 
5 
KEDNY and KEDLI, the Commission adopted a joint proposal providing 
6 
that any KEDNY or KEDLI customer who receives one or more of ten 
7 
public assistance programs would be eligible for the low income 
8 
assistance programs.  Continuing the eligibility of non-heating customers 
9 
for these programs is particularly important for low income customers 
10 
who rent rather than own their home or apartment, as many do in KEDNY 
11 
and KEDLI’s service territories.  Because low income non-heating 
12 
customers are already receiving these discounts, it would create 
13 
unacceptable rate shock for these customers to lose the discount, 
14 
particularly if the loss of benefits coincides with an increase in the bi-
15 
monthly minimum charge.  The volumetric nature of the discount already 
16 
provides a proportionately greater amount of assistance to low income gas 
17 
heating customers.   The Companies believe that the demographics of their 
18 
service territories warrants including non-heating customers in their 
19 
Residential Reduced Rate programs. 
20 
 
21 
70


--- Page 73 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel

Q. 
How do the Companies propose to revise their Reduced Residential 
1 
Rate programs?  
2 
A. 
 The Companies are proposing to increase the discounts to the monthly 
3 
customer charges and the second rate blocks (winter months only) for 
4 
heating and non-heating customers by five percent.  This will increase the 
5 
discount for low income heating customers from 56.52% to 61.52% of the 
6 
customer charge for KEDNY and from 83.19% to 88.19% for KEDLI.  
7 
The discount to the second rate blocks for the winter months will increase 
8 
from 49.61% to 54.61% for KEDNY and from 41.45% to 46.45% for 
9 
KEDLI.  For low income non-heating customers, the discount of the 
10 
monthly customer charge would increase from 21.83% to 26.83% for 
11 
KEDNY and 49.92% to 54.92% for KEDLI.  The Companies forecast the 
12 
total cost of the Residential Reduced Rate program to be $12.875 million 
13 
annually for KEDNY and $4.661 million for KEDLI, resulting in an 
14 
increase of $3.028 million above the current annual expenditures of 
15 
$9.847 million for KEDNY and of $1.338 million above the current 
16 
annual expenditures of $3.323 million for KEDLI.  The Companies’ Rate 
17 
Design Panels address how the Reduced Residential Rate programs will be 
18 
reflected in the rates of nonparticipating customers.   
19 
 
20

Q. 
What are the Companies’ projections of participation in their 
1 
Residential Reduced Rate programs? 
2 
A. 
Based on its current participation levels, KEDNY is forecasting 64,100 
3

4 
participants in the Historic Test Year.  Based on its current participation 
5 
levels, KEDLI is forecasting 13,900 participants, a decrease from the 
6 
target of 30,000 participants in the Historic Test Year.  The Companies are 
7 
convinced that more of their customers are in need of the benefits offered 
8 
by the programs; KEDLI in particular has struggled to enroll the number 
9 
of customers it projected.  This is why the Companies are exploring 
10 
automated enrollment to assist in reaching customers most in need.  The

Companies are working with New York City’s Human Resource 
12

13

customers in KEDNY’s service territory and the Rockaways portion of 
15 
KEDLI’s service territory.  KEDLI will also be working with the Office of 
16

17 
mechanism for its customers in Suffolk and Nassau Counties.  These file 
18 
sharing options could significantly increase the number of participants in 
19 
the Companies’ Reduced Rate Residential programs.

21 
72


--- Page 75 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel

For now, KEDNY and KEDLI believe maintaining their existing 
1 
automatic and manual enrollment mechanisms for the Residential Reduced 
2 
Rate programs is the best approach and the projected numbers of 
3 
participants do not reflect assumptions of increased participation as a 
4

5

6

7 
 
8 
Q. 
Please address the potential impacts of the filing sharing mechanisms 
9 
on participation in KEDNY and KEDLI’s Residential Reduced Rate 
10 
programs. 
11 
A. 
Based on the number of eligible low income customers identified by HRA 
12 
as eligible for Consolidated Edison’s low income program when it 
13 
implemented file sharing and a review of their own demographics, the 
14 
Companies would expect a very dramatic increase in the number of 
15 
participants and overall cost for their Residential Reduced Rate programs 
16 
from these file sharing mechanisms.  KEDNY estimates that the impact of 
17 
HRA file sharing could triple the cost of the Reduced Residential Rate 
18 
program per nonparticipating customer.  KEDLI estimates that an HRA 
19 
file match for the Rockaways portion of its service territory alone could 
20 
73


--- Page 76 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 72 of 88 
 
increase participants in its Reduced Rate Residential program by as many 
1 
as 6,000. 
2 
 
3 
 
These very significant potential costs warrant a thorough and deliberate 
4 
consideration of how to balance the interests of utilizing automated 
5 
enrollment to expand participation in the Companies’ programs with the 
6 
rate impacts on nonparticipants. 
7 
 
8 
As noted in Appendix D of Staff’s Straw Proposal, the annual cost of 
9 
KEDNY’s Reduced Residential Rate program for nonparticipating 
10 
customers is $11.00 per customer, which generally aligns with the average 
11 
for New York gas utilities of $12.80 per customer, while KEDLI’s 
12 
program costs are $9.81 per customer.  Significant increases to the number 
13 
of program participants would place a disproportionate burden on 
14 
KEDNY and KEDLI’s nonparticipating customers compared with other 
15 
utilities.  Accordingly, the Companies propose a collaborative with Staff, 
16 
HRA, OTDA and other interested parties to develop means of file 
17 
matching that limits, phases in and prioritizes the automatic enrollment of 
18 
new participants based on the greatest need.

75

Residential Reduced Rate Program would be eligible to receive a

9 
Q. 
Why do the Companies believe that a low income conversion rebate 
10 
and smart thermostat programs would be beneficial for low income 
11 
customers? 
12 
A. 
Many low income customers simply cannot afford the up-front costs to 
13 
convert their homes to natural gas.  As a result, these customers are 
14 
prevented from achieving the ongoing fuel savings that are associated with 
15 
converting to natural gas heat.  Similarly, smart thermostats can save on 
16 
energy bills by automatically adjusting temperatures and giving customers 
17 
more control over their energy use.  However, the upfront cost of 
18 
purchasing and installing a smart thermostat may prevent low income 
19 
customers from installing these devices.  The Companies’ proposed 
20 
programs are intended to assist low income customer in achieving these 
21 
76


--- Page 79 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 75 of 88 
 
benefits.  The programs have the added benefit of reducing greenhouse gas 
1 
emissions, thereby benefitting all customers and achieving one of the 
2 
Commission’s important policy objectives.   
3 
 
4

respectively.  A breakdown of program costs appears in Exhibit __ (SSP-

12).  These costs are reflected in the Companies’ Rate Year revenue

requirements.

circumstances.  The Companies believe that multiple, integrated solutions,

9 
VIII.  Paperless Billing 
10 
Q.    
Please describe the Companies’ paperless billing proposal.  
11 
A.  
The Companies are proposing to offer an incentive to customers to 
12 
encourage increased participation in paperless (also referred to as 
13 
electronic) billing.  This proposal is consistent with that proposed and 
14 
implemented by NMPC in its last rate case.  Customers who elect 
15 
electronic billing receive their bill electronically in lieu of a paper bill.  
16 
Under the paperless billing proposal, KEDNY customers electing this 
17 
option would receive a credit of $0.49 and KEDLI customers would 
18 
receive $0.35 on their bills to reflect the avoided cost of generating a paper 
19 
bill and issuing an electronic bill in its place (i.e., the cost of envelopes, 
20 
paper, postage and processing – less the cost of issuing an electronic bill).  
21 
78


--- Page 81 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 77 of 88 
 
Details on how the proposed credits are calculated are presented in 
1 
Exhibit___ (RDP-10), Schedule 3.  KEDNY is currently considering 
2 
updating its electronic billing options to provide additional functionality 
3 
for customers.  If this update occurs, it will impact the calculation of the 
4 
paperless billing credit, as further discussed in the testimony of the Rate 
5 
Design Panel.  As described in the testimony of the Revenue 
6 
Requirements Panels, to provide credits in the Rate Year to existing 
7 
electronically billed customers (e.g., those customers who elected to 
8 
receive electronic bills during the Historic Test Year), an adjustment to the 
9 
Historic Test Year was required to add back the avoided cost of issuing a 
10 
paper bill to those customers so that the cost can be passed back to them in 
11 
the Rate Year through an equal and offsetting credit.  
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Do the Companies expect savings from this program? 
14 
A. 
As described in the testimony of the Revenue Requirements Panels, the 
15 
program is intended to be cost and revenue neutral.  Savings achieved by 
16 
the Companies (i.e., the cost of envelopes, paper, postage, and processing 
17 
– less the cost to provide an electronic bill), would be returned to 
18 
electronically billed customers in the form of a credit on each bill.  
19 
 
20 
79


--- Page 82 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 78 of 88 
 
Q. 
Describe how the Companies intend to inform customers about the 
1 
paperless billing incentive. 
2 
A. 
The Companies intend to communicate with customers regarding the 
3 
paperless billing incentive using on-bill messages, bill inserts, and other 
4 
existing outreach channels, such as social media (e.g., Facebook, Twitter, 
5 
Web announcements), encouraging customers to switch to paperless 
6 
billing.  The Companies believe that focusing on these communication 
7 
channels is the most cost effective way to promote the program.  The 
8 
Companies are not proposing any incremental marketing or 
9 
communication expense for this program. 
10 
 
11 
IX. 
US Foundation Project and Enhancements 
12 
Q. 
Please describe the US Foundation Project (“USFP”). 
13 
A. 
In 2010, National Grid launched the USFP to consolidate systems onto a 
14 
single SAP enterprise resource platform (“ERP”) to replace functionality 
15 
delivered by the Oracle and PeopleSoft ERP Suites and associated 
16 
applications.  Following National Grid’s acquisition of KeySpan in 2007, 
17 
the US business was challenged in supporting multiple and complex 
18 
business processes that spanned multiple and complex technology 
19 
platforms. The fragmented business processes and systems created 
20 
numerous risks and challenges. Accordingly, National Grid and its 
21 
80


--- Page 83 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 79 of 88 
 
consultant partners designed and built a system to enable it to consolidate 
1 
National Grid’s Human Resources, Finance and Supply Chain processes 
2 
onto a single SAP platform. The conversion to the consolidated systems 
3 
was very complex and involved significant changes throughout National 
4 
Grid, which impacted technology, business processes and employees 
5 
across the US operation.  On November 5, 2012, National Grid launched 
6 
the majority of the SAP modules and business processes.  The remaining 
7 
modules and business processes were launched in December 2012.   
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What were the costs of USFP implementation? 
10 
A. 
The capital costs for the design, test, build and launch of USFP were 
11 
$223 million.  National Grid also incurred $126.8 million in operating 
12 
costs to design, build, test and launch USFP.  Those operating expenses 
13 
were recorded at National Grid USA’s parent, and therefore charged to 
14 
shareholders, not customers.   
15 
 
16 
Post implementation and prior to the Historic Test Year, National Grid 
17 
successfully implemented a number of system enhancements and added 
18 
new functionality. The purpose of these efforts was to enhance and 
19 
optimize the system to enable National Grid to more fully realize its 
20 
81


--- Page 84 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 80 of 88 
 
benefits.  National Grid took a conservative approach and only 
1 
capitalized costs incurred related to the development of new features, 
2 
functionality or modules in the SAP program that did not exist at the time 
3 
the software went live.  The approach for capitalizing these costs is 
4 
detailed in Attachment 3 of Exhibit __ (SSP-13).  Exhibit __(SSP-13) 
5 
includes relevant excerpts from the USFP Stabilization and Finance 
6 
Remediation Report (“Report”) provided to Department of Public 
7 
Service Staff on April 1, 2015 in response to a recommendation in the 
8 
recent gas management audit in Case 13-G-0009.     
9 
 
10 
The capital costs associated with USFP through fiscal year 2015 were 
11 
$313.4 million, which are summarized on Attachment 2 of Exhibit ___ 
12 
(SSP-13).    
13 
 
14 
Q. 
What USFP costs are currently included in rates? 
15 
A. 
KEDLI has not filed a revenue requirement since 2006 and thus no costs 
16 
associated with USFP implementation are currently being recovered from 
17 
KEDLI customers.  With respect to KEDNY, the Commission adopted a 
18 
Joint Proposal on June 13, 2013, in Case 12-G-0544, that extended 
19 
KEDNY’s rate plan with certain modifications (“Rate Plan Extension”).  
20 
KEDNY’s share of costs included in the Rate Plan Extension are set forth 
21 
82


--- Page 85 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 81 of 88 
 
in Attachment 1 of Exhibit ___ (SSP-13).    Specifically, the revenue 
1 
requirement in the Rate Plan Extension included KEDNY’s share of 
2 
USFP implementation costs, including approximately $275 million in 
3 
capital costs and approximately $3.7 million in post-implementation 
4 
support costs.   
5 
 
6 
Q.  
Please describe the USFP Implementation. 
7 
A. 
Recognizing the magnitude of the project, and that all companies 
8 
experience some degree of difficulty with projects of this size and scale, 
9 
National Grid expected implementation issues in the months following 
10 
conversion.  National Grid, however, experienced more difficulties than 
11 
were reasonably anticipated.  Initially, National Grid was unable to 
12 
timely and accurately pay many employees and vendors and struggled to 
13 
perform monthly financial close activities.  Because of these challenges, 
14 
National Grid was unable to meet certain financial and regulatory 
15 
reporting requirements on a timely basis.  In addition, the extensive data 
16 
validation and time-consuming manual workarounds required post-
17 
implementation aggravated pre-existing issues, including certain 
18 
deficiencies in internal controls over financial reporting, which National 
19 
Grid identified in 2013.   
20 
 
21 
83


--- Page 86 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 82 of 88 
 
Q. 
Please describe how National Grid stabilized the SAP system and is 
1 
addressing the controls deficiencies. 
2 
A. 
Given the volume and complexity of the system and process work 
3 
necessary, it was not possible to address all of the issues at one time and 
4 
it took nearly two years to fully stabilize the system.  The period from 
5 
mid-November 2012 through September 2014 is referred to as USFP 
6 
Stabilization.  Immediately following implementation, National Grid 
7 
retained hundreds of external resources to address the most critical issues 
8 
or develop manual alternatives.  National Grid then executed a 
9 
comprehensive plan comprised of system releases to stabilize the system.   
10 
 
11 
On January 1, 2014, National Grid successfully completed a system 
12 
release (referred to as Release 2), which was primarily focused on 
13 
enabling the Long Island Power Authority separation and addressing 
14 
critical human resources upgrades.  The second major milestone, and 
15 
critical path to stabilization, was the implementation of a system release 
16 
(referred to as Release 3), which was successfully launched on July 18, 
17 
2014.  The system work associated with the USFP Stabilization effort 
18 
was largely complete with the execution of Release 3, but performance 
19 
was closely monitored and measured through September 2014 to confirm 
20 
that Release 3 delivered the planned functionality and improvements.  
21 
84


--- Page 87 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 83 of 88 
 
National Grid confirmed in September 2014 that it had stabilized the 
1 
SAP system environment and substantially stabilized the associated 
2 
processes.  The time, work and costs of stabilizing the SAP system were 
3 
considerable; however, throughout this process, National Grid took steps 
4 
to ensure that all stabilization costs would be properly accounted for and 
5 
that none of the costs of stabilizing the system would be borne by 
6 
customers.  KEDNY and KEDLI are not seeking recovery of 
7 
stabilization costs in these proceedings.   
8 
 
9 
Concurrent with USFP Stabilization, National Grid has been executing a 
10 
comprehensive Finance Remediation Plan to address the deficiencies in 
11 
internal controls.  While National Grid has issued financial statements 
12 
with clean audit opinions every year, it recognizes that it must improve 
13 
its systems and processes to be able to fulfill all of its obligations on an 
14 
efficient, sustainable basis.  The Finance Remediation Plan has enabled 
15 
National Grid to improve its data quality, financial reporting and 
16 
controls.  Phase one of the Finance Remediation Plan is complete and 
17 
significant improvements have been achieved.  National Grid is also 
18 
executing a longer term plan to ensure this progress is sustained and to 
19 
drive continuous improvement.  
20 
 
21 
85


--- Page 88 ---
Testimony of Shared Services Panel 
Page 84 of 88 
 
Q.  
Please explain the costs of USFP Stabilization and the Finance 
1 
Remediation Plan. 
2 
A. 
The 
O&M 
costs 
associated 
with 
USFP 
Stabilization 
totaled 
3 
approximately $460.7 million.  Those costs were recorded at the National 
4 
Grid USA parent company and borne exclusively by shareholders.  The 
5 
incremental costs associated with the Finance Remediation Plan currently 
6 
total approximately $245 million and were similarly recorded at the 
7 
National Grid USA parent company.  Those costs were also borne 
8 
exclusively by shareholders.  The USFP Stabilization and Finance 
9 
Remediation Report details the process, review and analysis by which 
10 
National Grid ensured that USFP Stabilization and incremental Finance 
11 
Remediation Plan costs were charged to shareholders.  Importantly, all 
12 
USFP Stabilization costs incurred from mid-November 2012 through 
13 
September 2014 were recorded as O&M and charged to the National 
14 
Grid USA parent company.  The Report details National Grid’s 
15 
approach, post-stabilization, of capitalizing only those costs associated 
16 
with enhancements and upgrades to the system.  In addition, because the 
17 
USFP Stabilization work ceased in September 2014, prior to the 
18 
beginning of the Historic Test Year, there is further assurance that no 
19 
O&M associated with USFP Stabilization is embedded in KEDNY and 
20 
KEDLI’s Rate Year forecasts. 
21

Q. 
What enhancements or upgrades are planned to be in service after 
1 
the Historic Test Year through the Rate Year and Data Years? 
2 
A. 
In November 2015, National Grid successfully executed Enhancement 
3 
Release 1, which is the third and final major planned system release.  The 
4 
projects in Enhancement Release 1 delivered functionality incremental to 
5 
what was planned in the initial implementation of USFP.  For example, 
6 
the following enhancements and improvements were implemented: new 
7 
performance 
reporting 
capabilities; 
new 
supplier 
network 
and 
8 
improvements to inventory and warehouse management systems; 
9 
improved fleet end-to-end financial and inventory processes; and an 
10 
automated human resources benefits eligibility enrollment process. 
11 
 
12 
National Grid also plans to implement the FERC on HANA project in 
13 
March 2016.  National Grid will decommission the existing FERC 
14 
module. The new FERC solution will be supported by the HANA 
15 
architecture, allowing for real-time FERC reporting abilities.  The project 
16 
will improve the timeliness of mandatory FERC financial statements and 
17 
closing activity and support the Finance organization efforts to respond 
18 
quickly and effectively to numerous policy-driven, business, and 
19 
regulatory needs that arise over the course of any given year.

Inactive Accounts 
5 
Q. 
Are there any incremental positions needed to support the 
6 
Companies’ inactive accounts procedures? 
7 
A. 
As described in the testimony of KEDNY and KEDLI’s Gas 
8 
Infrastructure and Operations Panels, the Companies recently modified 
9 
their practices and procedures regarding inactive accounts.  In addition to 
10 
increased field operations costs, which are addressed by the GIOP 
11 
Panels, the Companies expect to fill 11 incremental positions to provide 
12 
back office support to the inactive accounts process.  These positions are 
13 
being created to assist the Companies with (i) identifying property 
14 
owners so the Companies can gain access to premises to secure the gas 
15 
meter and (ii) coordinate the legal replevin process if an owner cannot be 
16 
located.  The Rate Year costs for the 11 incremental positions are $0.163 
17 
million in labor and $0.084 million in labor adders (total $0.247 million) 
18 
for KEDNY and $0.036 million in labor and $0.023 million in labor 
19 
adders (total $0.059 million) for KEDLI.  (Exhibit ___ (RRP-3), 
20 
Schedule 27, Workpaper 9).

Re:   Case 06-M-0878 – Joint Petition of National Grid plc and KeySpan 
Corporation for Approval of Stock Acquisition and other Regulatory 
Authorizations 
 
Dear Secretary Brilling: 
 
The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY and KeySpan Gas East 
Corporation d/b/a National Grid (collectively, “National Grid” or “Companies”), hereby advise the 
Commission that the “Customer Service Quality Performance Requirements” of Sections V-B-3 
and VI-B-3 of the Joint Proposal for Gas Safety, Reliability and Customer Service Performance 
Requirements (“JP-3”), as adopted by the Commission in this proceeding,1 have been fully satisfied 
for calendar year 2009.  As evidence of the satisfaction of each of the standards, please find 
enclosed a detailed matrix reflecting both the benchmarks and actual performance.  
 
 Please contact the undersigned with any questions regarding this letter or the enclosure.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
 
Very truly yours, 
 
/s/ Mahati Guttikonda  
Mahati Guttikonda 
 
 
Encl. 
cc:  Catherine L. Nesser 
 
Adam L. Benshoff

(September 17, 2007), pp. 107-108 & 143 (adopting the August 23, 2007,  “Abbreviated Order”).

March 13, 2014 
 
 
Honorable Kathleen H. Burgess 
Secretary 
New York State Public Service Commission  
Three Empire State Plaza 
Albany, New York 12223 
 
Re: 
Case 06-M-0878 – Joint Petition of National Grid plc and KeySpan 
Corporation for Approval of Stock Acquisition and Other Regulatory 
Authorizations 
 
Dear Secretary Burgess: 
 
 
KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) submits a 
correction to its 2012 Customer Service Quality Performance Requirements report.  The 
correction is due to a transcription error in the monthly Residential Customer Satisfaction 
scores.  Attachment 1 shows the corrected monthly and year-end Residential Customer 
Satisfaction scores.  Attachment 2 is the corrected 2012 annual report.  Notwithstanding 
the correction, KEDLI still achieved its Residential Customer Satisfaction performance 
target in 2012.   
 
 
Should you have any questions or concerns, please do not hesitate to contact me.  
Thank you for your time and attention. 
 
 
 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
 
/s/ Patric R. O’Brien  
 
Patric R. O’Brien  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Patric R. O’Brien 
Senior Counsel 
40 Sylvan Road, Waltham, MA 02451

Re: 
Case 06-M-0878 – Joint Petition of National Grid plc and KeySpan 
Corporation for Approval of Stock Acquisition and Other Regulatory 
Authorizations 
 
Case 12-G-0544 - In the Matter of the Commission's Examination of The 
Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY's Earnings 
Computation Provisions and Other Continuing Elements of the Applicable 
Rate Plan. 
 
Dear Secretary Burgess: 
 
Pursuant to the Joint Proposal for Gas Safety, Reliability and Customer Service 
Performance Requirements (“JP-3) in Case 06-M-0878, KeySpan Gas East Corporation 
d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) and The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National 
Grid NY (“KEDNY”) submits for filing the 2013 Customer Service Quality Performance 
Requirements report.   
 
KEDNY met all of its customer service requirements in 2013.  KEDLI, however, 
missed its customer satisfaction target largely due to the impacts from Super Storm 
Sandy.  KEDLI will be submitting a petition shortly to address the 2013 customer 
satisfaction target.   
 
 
 
Should you have any questions or concerns, please do not hesitate to contact me.  
Thank you for your time and attention. 
 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
 
/s/ Patric R. O’Brien  
 
Patric R. O’Brien

Re:

Case 12-G-0544 - In the Matter of the Commission's Examination of The 
Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY's Earnings 
Computation Provisions and Other Continuing Elements of the Applicable 
Rate Plan. 
 
Dear Secretary Burgess: 
 
Pursuant to the Joint Proposal for Gas Safety, Reliability and Customer Service 
Performance Requirements (“JP-3) in Case 06-M-0878, KeySpan Gas East Corporation 
d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) and The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National 
Grid NY (“KEDNY”) submits for filing the 2014 Customer Service Quality Performance 
Requirements report.   
 
KEDNY met all of its customer service requirements in 2014.  KEDLI, however, 
missed its customer satisfaction target largely due to the impacts of a system conversion 
and high bills caused by the extreme cold weather.  KEDLI also missed its complaint rate 
metric.   
 
 
 
Should you have any questions or concerns, please do not hesitate to contact me.  
Thank you for your time and attention. 
 
 
Respectfully submitted, 
 
 
/s/ Patric R. O’Brien

  Potential Offset = 1 standard deviation above historical mean.
