Exhibit __ (SSP-10) 
 
Illustrations of the Service Quality Thresholds, Floor, Offset Mechanism 
 
When performance falls below the threshold for a metric, the associated negative 
revenue adjustment is incurred, subject to offset.  So long as performance is between threshold 
and floor, the negative revenue adjustment is subject to offset.   The amount of each threshold, 
corresponding negative revenue adjustment, and potential offset opportunities are shown in 
Exhibit __ (SSP-9).  
 
The floor for each metric is set at a level where performance has declined to the point 
where it would not be considered subject to offset.  This provides a financial incentive for the 
company to keep performance above the floor for all metrics, regardless of offset opportunities.  
  
During each annual performance period, the total negative revenue adjustments owed by 
the company would be equal to the negative revenue adjustments for metrics below the penalty 
floor (which cannot be offset); plus the total negative revenue adjustments between penalty 
threshold and penalty floor (i.e., subject to offset); minus the sum of any achieved offsets.  If the 
company achieves the offset level on a metric but has met all thresholds (i.e., no negative 
revenue adjustments have been incurred), there is no positive revenue adjustment.   
 
If the company achieves an Incentive-Only metric, the company will receive a positive 
revenue adjustment for that amount.  However, this would be reduced by the amount of any 
negative revenue adjustments incurred during the period.  
 
Illustrating examples (for KEDLI, the same approach would apply to KEDNY): 
 
 
If KEDLI achieves 72.0% contact center service level (performance above the offset 
level) and achieves performance above all penalty thresholds, there will be no positive 
revenue adjustment. 
 
 
If the KEDLI appointments kept performance falls below 90.3% (threshold) there will be a 
negative revenue adjustment of $1.98 million. This negative revenue adjustment is 
subject to offset by high performance on one or more other metrics, which achieve the 
penalty offset value.  
 
 
If the KEDLI appointments kept performance falls below 87.9% (penalty floor) the 
negative revenue adjustment of $1.98 million will be incurred.  If during the same period 
performance on contact center is 72% (exceeding offset level), there will be no offset, 
since the company cannot mitigate the $1.98 million negative revenue adjustment for 
performance below the penalty floor for appointments kept. 
 
 
If KEDLI appointments kept performance is 89.0% (below the penalty threshold) and 
contact center service level is 72% (above offset level), a negative revenue adjustment of 
$1.98 million would be incurred for appointments kept, which would be reduced by the 
$0.50 million offset for performance on the contact center service level, resulting in a 
$1.48 million negative revenue adjustment.  The negative revenue adjustment in this 
example could be further increased by another missed penalty threshold or further 
reduced by achieving another offset metric.   
 
 
To illustrate what occurs (e.g., for KEDLI) when multiple negative revenue adjustments 
and multiple offset occur during the same period: 
 
o 
appointments kept = 87.9% (below the penalty floor), resulting in a $1.98 million 
negative revenue adjustment;  
180


--- Page 194 ---
Exhibit_(SSP-10)  
  Illustration of Service Quality Metrics 
 
Page 2 of 2 
  
o 
contact center service level = 62% (between penalty threshold and penalty floor), 
resulting in a $.99 million negative revenue adjustment;  
o 
satisfaction with service provided = 89% (above the offset level), an offset of 
$0.50 million would be achieved; and  
o 
adjusted bills is below 0.54% (meeting the offset level), an offset of $0.99 million 
would be achieved; then 
o 
The $0.50 million offset for satisfaction and $0.99 million offset for adjusted bills 
would completely offset the contact center service level negative revenue 
adjustment of $0.99 million.  However, the $1.98 million negative revenue 
adjustment (for performance below the penalty floor) is not reduced by any 
offsets, resulting in a total negative revenue adjustment of $1.98 million.  Even 
though the offsets in this example ($1.49 million) are well in excess of the 
negative revenue adjustments subject to offset ($0.99 million), the additional 
offset opportunity ($0.50 million) cannot be applied to performance below the 
penalty floor, and the company would incur the full $1.98 million for missed 
performance on appointments kept. 
 
 
If KEDLI performance on the incentive-only percentage of payments made through the 
web achieves 11.6% or above, the company would receive a positive revenue adjustment 
of $.99M.  This amount would be subject to reduction if negative revenue adjustments 
were incurred during the same period. 
 
 
If the company meets one or both of the Incentive metrics, the negative revenue 
adjustment owed by the company would be reduced by the amount of any positive 
revenue adjustments achieved. To illustrate, if KEDLI’s IVR self-service rate falls below 
21.7% (with a negative revenue adjustment of $1.49 million), but KEDLI achieves the 
percent of payments through web and mobile incentive (with a $0.99 million incentive), 
the positive incentive would be subtracted from the negative revenue adjustment, and the 
company would owe the balance of $0.50 million as a negative revenue adjustment.

Work Papers for Service Quality Metrics

Metric
Definition
Level Setting Methodology
Percent Total bills 
Adjusted/Billing Accuracy
“Adjusted Customer Bills” is a measure of the proportion of customer bills that require later 
adjustment due to errors by KEDNY / KEDLI, expressed as a percentage of total customer bills.  
Adjusted Customer Bills will be measured monthly and the performance incentive mechanism 
will be based on the average of the twelve monthly scores.  Following recommendations of the 
NY PSC Customer Service Data Audit (Case 13-M-0314), a June 25, 2015 Adjusted Bills Work 
Group summary defined the following situations which will not constitute an adjustment due to 
company error (and are thus excluded):
 • an estimated bill replaced by a bill based on an actual reading
 • a customer reading replaced with an actual or estimated reading
 • a change in a customer bill due to switching to or from budget billing
 • changes to correct for the responsible billing party (e.g., where bills have been rendered to a 
prior resident, and bills are re-issued to the current resident)
The company has calculated new targets based upon the definitions of the Adjusted Bills Work 
Group summary and recent actual performance benchmarks.
Threshold (target) = 3 year avg, floor = .2 less 
than threshold, offset = .2 above highest of CY 
12,13, 14 or 15 YTD.  KEDLI targets further 
reduced by .11% to normalize for future inclusion 
of estimate->actual conditioner (average 
difference Jan-Jun 2015).  KEDNY targets further 
increased by .15% to normalize for historical 
change from Overland audit findings (average 
difference July-Dec 2014).
Payment Processing
Payment processing is a new metric that measures exception payments that result from 
processing or procedural exceptions due to company error.  The metric is a measure of the 
proportion of exceptions due to company error, per 100,000 payments processed. 
There were only eleven months of data available 
to determine thresholds for the payment 
processing metric.  To increase the level of 
confidence that the metric is set at an appropriate 
level, the Company proposes to continue to 
monitor performance and propose targets based 
on the results for the 23 months ending December 
2016.  Proposed targets are expected to follow the 
approach of 2σ below mean for threshold, floor at 
3σ below mean, and offset at 1σ above mean.
Percent Appointments kept
The appointments kept metric is currently a performance indicator with the following revised 
definition:  An appointment will now be defined as any scheduled meeting between the Company 
and the customer (or customer’s representative) at the customer’s premises in which a date and 
time or timeframe is agreed upon by both parties.  The metric will apply to appointments made 
and performed by the Company’s Customer Meter Services (“CMS”) organization, which 
constitute more than 95 percent of appointments performed by KEDNY and KEDLI employees.  
Examples of appointments included in the metric are meter reads, turn-ons or offs, and meter 
changes.  Under the proposed metric, an appointment is considered kept if the Company 
satisfies the terms of the appointment (date, time, and location).  In addition, an appointment is 
considered kept (i) if the customer is unavailable at the premises when the Company arrives 
within the scheduled appointment window, or (ii) the customer is available at the premises 
before the scheduled window with prior communication of the adjusted time made to the 
customer.  The proposed definition takes into account discussions of the ongoing working group 
in the Focused Operations Audit of Utilities’ Reported Data (Case 13-M-0314).
Offset = 1σ above mean, threshold = 2σ below 
mean, and floor = 3σ below mean.  Levels are 
based on historic appointment metrics and 
performance would need to be re-baselined if 
appointment definitions/classifications change
IVR self-service rate
This is a new metric measuring the percent of customers using the IVR that are able to complete 
their task through the automated system
Offset = 1σ above mean, penalty threshold = 2σ 
below mean, and penalty floor = 3σ below mean
Contact Center Service 
Level
No change to the current performance metric is proposed for KEDNY.  This is a new metric for 
KEDLI.  A new target was determined for KEDNY based on historical data, and the service level 
for KEDNY was used to establish the threshold for KEDLI.  Service level is measured as percent 
of calls answered by a live customer service agent in less than 30 seconds. Calls handled and 
abandoned in the IVR are not part of the service level calculation.
KEDNY offset = 1σ above mean, penalty threshold 
=  historic mean, and penalty floor = 1σ below 
mean.  To align performance metrics, KEDLI 
threshold, floor and offset are set to the more 
stringent KEDNY levels. 
Satisfaction with customer 
service provided
No change is proposed to the current definitions and targets for customer satisfaction for 
KEDNY and KEDLI.  The Companies are proposing a penalty floor at 2σ below the existing 
penalty threshold levels and potential offset at 1σ above the existing threshold. The Companies 
are investigating moving to a new metric consistent with the methodology used by Niagara 
Mohawk.  The Niagara Mohawk methodology rates satisfaction, via % answering 8, 9, or 10, to 
the following question: “28) Overall, on a scale from 1 to 10, where 1 means Dissatisfied and 10 
means satisfied, how satisfied are you with the services provided by National Grid?" 
Offset = 1σ above current metric level, threshold = 
existing metric levels, and floor = 2σ below 
existing metric level
Escalated Complaints
This is an existing metric – with no change to definition.  This metric measures the number of 
escalated complaints per 100K Customers.   For KEDLI, the approach of using historical 
performance less two standard deviations would result a reduced standard (1.35 complaints per 
100,000 customers) vs the current target of 1.1 complaints.  Thus, KEDLI proposes to retain the 
existing more stringent standard of 1.1 complaints per 100,000 customers.  KEDNY's historical 
data, less two standard deviations results in a new, more rigorous standard of 1.05 complaints 
per 100,000 customers.  
KEDLI: Offset =  1σ above mean, threshold = 
existing target of 1.1, and penalty floor = 3σ below 
mean.  KEDNY: Offset = 1σ above existing metric, 
penalty threshold = 2σ below existing metric, and 
penalty floor = 3σ below existing metric.   
Performance targets are contingent upon adding 2 
FTEs to handle escalated complaints, one for each 
company.
Percent of payments made 
through Web & Mobile
Low Income Outreach and 
Assistance Programs
Incentive Only Metrics
New metric (incentive) measuring payments received through 1 Time Payment on Website, Mobile Website or Mobile App as a percentage of total 
payments received.
This is a new incentive metric measuring the number of low income program participants assisted with a KEDNY or KEDLI billing matter or referred to a 
health and human service agency by a Consumer Advocate at an outreach event.

 I. 
INTRODUCTION 
 
On October 2, 2014, the New York State Public Service Commission issued the audit report 
prepared by NorthStar Consulting Group (“NorthStar”) regarding its Comprehensive 
Management and Operations Audit of National Grid USA’s (“National Grid” or the “parent”) 
New York Gas Companies.  In response, the Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National 
Grid NY (“KEDNY”), KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“KEDLI”) and 
Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (“NMPC”) (collectively, the “NY 
Operating Companies”) submitted an implementation plan dated November 3, 2014 
(“Implementation Plan”).  The NY Operating Companies submit this report pursuant to the 
Implementation Plan and specifically in response to NorthStar’s recommendation IV-1. 
 
II. 
NorthStar’s Recommendation  
Recommendation IV-1 
Prepare a report for submittal to the PSC staff within six months that fully documents the capital 
and O&M costs associated with USFP, USFP Stabilization, Finance Remediation and other 
financial and IS system related initiatives so that ratepayers are protected from SAP-related costs 
in excess of levels agreed upon in the previous NMPC and KEDNY rate cases (and for KEDLI 
by default).  At a minimum, NG USA should: 
• Document and re-establish the original capital and O&M costs underlying the rate case 
level. 
• Clearly and specifically define the distinctions (e.g., timing, scope and cost) between the 
original USFP project, the USFP Stabilization Project, other SAP/USFP-related projects, 
the Finance Remediation Project and other financial and IS system activities. 
• Specify how the costs associated with each of these activities are being tracked, 
monitored and verified, including: the specific project or cost codes used for dedicated 
internal labor, part-time or as-needed internal labor, contractors, other direct, indirect 
and capital costs, and the procedures for review, verification, challenge and correction of 
costs.  
• Explain and document the impact of back-filling positions assigned to these initiatives. 
• Explain and document the sources of the increase in “other IS” capital and O&M 
expenditures (see Exhibit IV-11) to confirm the costs are not related to the USFP 
Stabilization initiatives. 
 
The purpose of the report is to provide the PSC staff with contemporaneous documentation so 
the costs for fixing USFP/SAP will not be borne by ratepayers, and the costs of Finance 
Remediation and other IS issues can be appropriately assessed in future rate cases.  National 
Grid would continue to have the right to justify costs for SAP enhancements and other initiatives 
for inclusion in rates as a part of the normal rate case process. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 3 of 44
194


--- Page 211 ---

III. 
National Grid’s Implementation Plan 
Implementation Plan Leads 
Executive Sponsor: 
Vivienne Bracken, SVP – Shared Services 
Team Lead(s): 
Rachel Patton – Finance 
 
 
Implementation Priority: High     
 
 
 
Recommendation Accepted: Yes (with modifications) 
 
Proposal to Implement Recommendation  
National Grid takes very seriously its commitment that customers will not bear the incremental 
costs associated with stabilizing its SAP system or the Finance Remediation Plan and the 
Company generally accepts the reporting requirement described by NorthStar in this 
recommendation.  However, the Company believes that limited modification to the scope of the 
report is appropriate.   
 
Consistent with NorthStar’s recommendation, the Company will identify the capital and O&M 
costs associated with USFP, USFP Stabilization, incremental Finance Remediation costs and the 
capital costs associated with other IS system-related initiatives.  Specifically, the Company will 
(i) identify the original capital and O&M costs associated with USFP as currently included in 
rates; (ii) identify actual O&M and capital costs associated with USFP, USFP Stabilization, and 
incremental Finance Remediation costs; (iii) explain the distinctions (i.e., timing, scope and cost) 
among the original USFP project, the USFP Stabilization Project, and the Finance Remediation 
Project; and (iv) explain how the costs associated with each of these activities are being tracked, 
monitored and verified.   
 
With respect to USFP Stabilization and Finance Remediation, the Company will include: the 
accounting treatment for the projects and any guidance provided by management regarding 
treatment of dedicated internal labor, part-time or as-needed internal labor, contractors, other 
direct, indirect and capital costs, and the procedures for review, verification, challenge and 
correction of costs.   
 
With respect to other IS expenditures, information will be provided to demonstrate that the 
increase in rent expense is not the result of including USFP Stabilization costs.  Specifically, 
the Company will describe the projects underlying Exhibit IV-10, identify the capital and O&M 
costs associated with these projects and explain why rent expense is increasing.  The Company 
will describe the process for charging IS capital and O&M costs.  The Company is not 
proposing to include all general IS O&M costs in the report as the Company is reviewing all 
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 4 of 44
195


--- Page 212 ---
5 
 
costs associated with vendors supporting stabilization and remediation to ensure that those costs 
have been properly recorded.  Similarly, the Company is reviewing all internal labor costs 
associated with employees supporting those efforts.  Accordingly, the possibility of 
stabilization or remediation costs having been reflected in general IS O&M costs that could be 
recovered from customers is extremely remote and likely de minimis.  The Company submits 
that a complete review of all general IS O&M would be an inefficient use of significant 
resources.  Further, NorthStar’s primary concern appears to be the increase in capital 
expenditures, which will be captured in the Company’s report. 
 
Schedule 
 
The Company will submit the report no later than April 1, 2015. 
 
In preparing the report, certain accounting adjustments may be identified as appropriate.  The 
Company will make necessary adjustments no later than 30 days after submission of the report. 
 
The Company will meet with Staff to discuss the report within 60 days of submission of the 
report. 
 
Summary of Cost/Benefit and Risk Analysis 
 
NorthStar estimates that the report will require 80 hours of a financial analyst (at $150,000 fully 
loaded), 40 hours of manager level (at $300,000), and 40 hours of manager or director level (at 
$350,000) for a total estimated cost of $19,000. 
 
Provided the scope of the report is as described in the proposal to implement the 
recommendation, NorthStar’s cost/ benefit and risk analysis is reasonable.  Absent the 
modification to the scope; however, NorthStar’s assessment is significantly understated with 
respect to the time and resources required to prepare the report.   
 
Measures of Success 
 
• Timely submission of the report. 
• Completion of any required accounting adjustments. 
• Meeting held with Staff to discuss the report.

IV. 
USFP Stabilization and Finance Remediation Report 
A. 
Background 
During NorthStar’s audit and in several updates to Department of Public Service Staff (“Staff”), 
National Grid has detailed the significant challenges it faced post implementation of its new back 
office system (“SAP”) and the work it is doing to address internal control deficiencies relating to 
certain financial processes.  The purpose of this report is to provide assurance that the 
incremental costs associated with these efforts are being absorbed by shareholders and thus will 
not be borne by customers.  In addition, this report describes other IS projects to demonstrate 
that the year on year increase in depreciation expense is the result of independent projects 
unrelated to the SAP implementation challenges that the Company undertook to improve service 
to customers. 
For purposes of this report, National Grid’s effort to develop and implement a new back office 
system is referred to as the US Foundation Project (“USFP”); the effort to address the post 
implementation issues, which occurred from mid-November 2012 through September 2014, is 
referred to as USFP Stabilization; and the effort to address control deficiencies in certain 
financial processes is referred to as the Finance Remediation Plan.  Section IV-B of this report 
summarizes the scope and timing of these efforts, identifies the costs, explains the accounting 
treatment and supports any capitalization of costs, where applicable.  Section IV-C sets forth 
the other IS projects and associated costs that are unrelated to USFP Stabilization and the 
Finance Remediation Plan. 
 B. 
Projects 
 
1. 
USFP  
In 2010, the Company launched the USFP to consolidate systems onto a single SAP ERP 
platform to replace functionality delivered by the Oracle and PeopleSoft ERP Suites and 
associated applications.  Following National Grid’s acquisition of KeySpan in 2007, the US 
business was challenged in supporting multiple and complex business processes that spanned 
multiple and complex technology platforms.  The fragmented business processes and systems 
created numerous risks and challenges.  Accordingly, National Grid retained consultants to 
design and build a system to enable it to consolidate its Human Resources, Finance and Supply 
Chain processes on a single platform.  For purposes of this report, USFP includes the time 
period to design, build, test and implement the system and associated processes, which began in 
2010 and ceased upon implementation of the majority of system applications in November 2012. 
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 6 of 44
197


--- Page 214 ---
7 
 
In the 2012 Niagara Mohawk Electric and Gas Rate Case, Niagara Mohawk submitted detailed 
information regarding the actual and forecast costs associated with the implementation of USFP.1  
Those costs were thoroughly reviewed in the rate case and Niagara Mohawk is currently 
recovering its share of total forecast USFP capital costs of approximately $274 million.2  Please 
see Attachment 1, Summary Schedule, for a summary of the USFP related costs currently 
embedded in Niagara Mohawk rates.  Behind the Summary Schedule in Attachment 1 are the 
exhibits from the rate case that detail the amounts in Niagara Mohawk rates.  With respect to 
KEDNY, the Commission approved a Joint Proposal on June 13, 2013 that extended KEDNY’s 
rate plan with certain modifications (“Rate Plan Extension”).3  The revenue requirement in the 
Rate Plan Extension included KEDNY’s share of USFP costs, which are set forth in Attachment 
1.  National Grid has not filed a revenue requirement for KEDLI since rates were set in 2008.   
Please see Attachment 2 for the actual capital and operating expenses associated with 
implementation of USFP.4  In addition, as shown in Attachment 2 and described in section 
IV-B3 below, the Company has made certain capital investments in the system to enhance and 
add new functionality.  Detailed support for capitalized USFP costs is provided in Attachment 
3, USFP Capital Expenditure Analysis.  
  
 
2. 
USFP Stabilization 
 
 
a. 
Background 
As detailed in NorthStar’s report, National Grid experienced significant post implementation 
challenges.  Initially, National Grid focused on the most critical issues and retained hundreds of 
external resources to address the issues or develop manual alternatives.  To track the costs 
associated with stabilization efforts, employees working on stabilization from mid-November 
until the following summer charged orders that had been established for USFP.  All costs from 
mid-November 2012 through March 31, 2013, including internal labor and consultants, were 
                                                           
1 Cases 12-E-0201 & 12-G-0202, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission as to the Rates, Charges, Rules 
and Regulations of Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid, Order Approving Electric 
and Gas Rate Plans in Accord with the Joint Proposal (issued and effective March 15, 2013).  
2 This forecast included approximately 90 days of post implementation support and certain functionality 
that was planned to be implemented subsequent to the initial launch of the system.   
3 Case 12-G-0544, In the Matter of the Commission's Examination of The Brooklyn Union Gas Company 
d/b/a National Grid NY's Earnings Computation Provisions and Other Continuing Elements of the 
Applicable Rate Plan, Order Adopting Terms of a Joint Proposal (issued and effective June 13, 2013). 
4 It should be noted that the costs reflected in Attachment 2 for the design, build, test and implementation of 
USFP are the actual costs incurred.  Those costs do not include 90 days of post implementation support or 
the subsequently planned functionality.  Accordingly, the actual costs presented in the column for the 
design, build, test and implementation of USFP do not directly correlate to the forecast costs in Attachment 
1. 
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 7 of 44
198


--- Page 215 ---
8 
 
moved to operating expense and charged to the parent even if those costs had initially been 
capitalized.  This was done to ensure that shareholders bore all stabilization costs.  The 
Company then performed a detailed review and only those costs meeting the conservative 
standard detailed in Attachment 3 were subsequently capitalized.  In addition, as described 
below, the Company reviewed every invoice from each vendor supporting stabilization efforts to 
ensure that those costs were allocated to orders charged to shareholders and not customers.   
By the summer of 2013, a number of the most critical implementation issues had been mitigated 
and National Grid established a team (referred to as the USFP Business Improvement team) to 
ensure accountability and develop an integrated plan for addressing the outstanding issues 
identified post SAP implementation.  Given the volume and complexity of system and process 
work to be completed, however, it was not possible to address all the issues and improvements at 
one time.  Resource, time, system and cost constraints demanded a disciplined review of the 
scope of work to be completed and the team took a phased approach to implementing the 
necessary system and process changes.  Each phase of that work is referred to as a system 
release.5  Release 2 was successfully completed on January 1, 2014 and was primarily focused 
on enabling the LIPA separation and addressing critical human resources upgrades.  In July 
2014, Release 3, which was designed to achieve stabilization, was successfully implemented.  
The system work associated with the USFP Stabilization effort was largely complete with the 
execution of Release 3, but performance was closely monitored and measured through 
September to confirm that Release 3 delivered the planned functionality and improvements.  
When it ascertained that it had, the Company confirmed in September 2014 that it had stabilized 
the SAP system environment and substantially stabilized the associated processes.  The 
incremental costs to achieve stabilization are set forth in Attachment 2. 
 
 
b. 
Tracking and Verification of Costs 
Throughout each phase of USFP Stabilization, the Company has taken care to ensure that costs 
are accounted for properly.  Specifically, stabilization costs are primarily comprised of 
consultant expense.  To ensure appropriate controls were in place and shareholders absorbed the 
costs, contractor invoices were charged to specially created purchase orders with accounting that 
directed costs to the parent.  The USFP Business Improvement team established strict 
governance around the payment of contractor invoices.  Company management reviewed these 
invoices to determine accuracy and payments were not made absent approval.  In addition, a 
comprehensive review of all contractor invoices from mid-November 2012 through September 
2014 was performed to further ensure that no stabilization costs were charged to the operating 
companies.  Specifically, the Company identified the contractors that supported the USFP 
Stabilization efforts and extracted accounts payable data relating to those contractors and sorted 
it by order.  The orders that were clearly related to USFP Stabilization and charged to the parent 
                                                           
5 Enhancement phases were also developed, but are beyond the scope of this report.  
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 8 of 44
199


--- Page 216 ---
9 
 
were excluded from the review, as the accounting was proper.  Other orders charged to the 
parent were likewise excluded as irrelevant.  All remaining invoices were reviewed as follows: 
• Each invoice was examined to determine the nature of the work performed; 
• Where there was insufficient information to determine the nature of the work 
performed, the employee who approved the invoice for payment was contacted 
for additional information; and 
• Any USFP Stabilization invoice that was not charged to the parent was identified 
for correction. 
    
Attachment 4 is a summary of the analysis performed and sets forth the vendors reviewed, 
number of invoices, and dollars reviewed.6  The Company reviewed more than 4,000 invoices 
totaling nearly $270 million.7  The Company found an error rate of 0.19% of dollars that should 
have been charged to the parent.  Attachment 4 sets forth the adjustment to address the 
identified errors. 
With respect to internal labor, internal orders were established to track incremental costs 
associated with stabilization efforts incurred post establishment of the USFP Business 
Improvement team.  The orders and associated accounting treatment are set forth in Attachment 
5.  Employees who were dedicated to USFP Stabilization efforts and no longer maintaining 
their previous day-to-day responsibilities charged USFP Stabilization-specific internal orders to 
ensure their costs were borne by the parent and not customers.  Employees supporting 
stabilization efforts part time in addition to their day-to-day responsibilities or in otherwise 
enduring roles were instructed to charge all companies for their work on USFP Stabilization.  
These employees do not reflect incremental costs to address stabilization efforts because their 
costs would have been incurred regardless of their efforts on stabilization work.  The internal 
labor associated with stabilization efforts is being reviewed.  Specifically, the project and work 
stream leads have been provided a list of employees who charged the stabilization orders.  The 
leads are verifying that the employees charged their time accurately and that the appropriate time 
was charged to the parent company.  In reviewing the internal labor, it is clear that some 
employees erred on the side of caution and charged time to the parent even where they were 
supporting stabilization efforts in addition to their day-to-day responsibilities.  No internal labor 
costs were capitalized post implementation.  It is important to note that USFP Stabilization 
work ceased in September 2014 and, regardless how time was charged, the costs associated with 
internal labor supporting stabilization will not be recovered from customers, as those costs were 
incurred prior to any test year.   
                                                           
6 As discussed in section 4 below, National Grid performed the same review of invoices for vendors who 
supported the Finance Remediation Plan.  Attachment 4 is a comprehensive list of all vendor invoices 
reviewed for USFP Stabilization and Finance Remediation. 
7 This amount excludes invoices charged to the parent.  It also excludes amounts charged to USFP capital 
as those costs were reviewed in the analysis described in Attachment 3.   
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 9 of 44
200


--- Page 217 ---
10 
 
 
3. 
Enhancements and New Functionality  
Concurrent with efforts to stabilize the system by correcting defects and errors, National Grid 
successfully implemented a number of system enhancements and added new system 
functionality.  For example, the Company added functionality to enable the processing of 
off-cycle payroll, which was not part of the initial implementation. 
The implementation of enhancements or additional functionality is not stabilization work.  
Accordingly, the costs associated with these enhancements and additional functionality were 
capitalized, consistent with US GAAP accounting standards.  Recognizing the need to be 
vigilant in assuring that stabilization costs are absorbed by shareholders, National Grid 
established a robust process for tracking and reviewing costs for possible capitalization.   
Indeed, the Company took a very conservative approach to what should be capitalized.  See 
Attachment 3 for detailed support of the capitalized costs associated with USFP from inception 
through September 2014.8  Attachment 3 also details the process utilized to analyze proper 
accounting treatment.   
 
4. 
Finance Remediation   
In 2013 and 2014, National Grid identified certain deficiencies in areas including its internal 
controls over financial reporting, which were confirmed in letters from National Grid’s 
independent auditor acknowledging material weaknesses and significant deficiencies in its 
control framework.  In response, National Grid developed a comprehensive Finance 
Remediation Plan.  Immediately, the Company implemented a number of interventions, which 
were designed to ensure that appropriate processes were in place to produce accurate financial 
data.  These process interventions are effective, but inefficient.  In the longer term, the Finance 
Remediation Plan will enable National Grid to implement sustainable controls that will facilitate 
the efficient production of accurate and timely data and financial reporting.   
In certain instances, USFP implementation challenges contributed to or resulted in controls 
deficiencies; however, many existed prior to the implementation of SAP.  Accordingly, the 
Company’s Finance Remediation efforts were coordinated with USFP Stabilization efforts, but 
executed independently.  The Finance Remediation effort was led and managed by an 
independent team with a finance lead participating in USFP Stabilization as well to ensure close 
coordination.  The Finance Remediation effort is specific to developing appropriate internal 
controls.  System issues are addressed in the context of USFP Stabilization efforts or 
enhancement projects. 
Similar to USFP Stabilization, management provided guidance regarding how costs should be 
charged.  Specifically, consultant and contractor costs associated with the Finance Remediation 
                                                           
8 Although beyond the scope of this report, it should be noted that the Company continues to develop and 
implement enhancement projects to more fully realize the benefits of the system. 
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 10 of 44
201


--- Page 218 ---
11 
 
effort are charged to the parent to ensure that those costs are not borne by customers.  
Employees in Finance were provided guidance to charge their time to project specific orders. 
Attachment 6 provides a list of those orders.  Nearly all employees in Finance supported the 
project part time as part of their day-to-day responsibilities and will return to their typical role 
when their assignments are complete.  Accordingly, the orders established to track their time 
allocated costs to all companies consistent with cost allocation principles.  Employees outside 
of Finance (for example, employees in Regulatory) who supported the effort in addition to their 
day-to-day responsibilities were directed to likewise charge their time to all companies.   
The Company has taken care to ensure that shareholders absorb the incremental costs associated 
with Finance Remediation.  With respect to contractor and consultant costs, at the outset, 
purchase orders were established with the vendors supporting the Finance Remediation Plan.  
The accounting associated with those purchase orders directed that all costs be charged to the 
parent thereby ensuring that these costs would be absorbed by shareholders.  Every invoice 
from the consultants and contractors is reviewed and approved by a management employee 
responsible for the services being provided and no invoice is paid until after it is approved.  In 
addition, contractor and consultant costs are reviewed monthly by the Finance Decision Support 
team to ensure proper accounting and treatment of the costs.  Further, the review of contractor 
and consultant invoices described in the USFP Stabilization section above was also performed 
with respect to all vendors supporting Finance Remediation efforts.  Accordingly, all paid 
invoices associated with Finance Remediation and USFP Stabilization have been reviewed to 
assure proper accounting treating as summarized in Attachment 4.  
With respect to internal labor costs, the Company requested that each work stream lead on the 
project review the employees and hours charged to their area of responsibility.  Those leads are 
reviewing the time charged to orders by specific project to ensure that time was reasonably and 
accurately charged.  Similar to the review of labor costs associated with USFP Stabilization, it 
is clear that some employees erred on the side of caution and charged time to the parent even 
where they were supporting Finance Remediation efforts in addition to their day-to-day 
responsibilities.   
No Finance Remediation costs have been capitalized and operating expense costs associated with 
the project that were incurred outside a test year will not be borne by customers.  Importantly, 
incremental costs associated with the effort continue to be charged to the parent and thus will not 
be borne by customers, regardless of when they were incurred.  
Attachment 7 sets forth the incremental costs associated with the Finance Remediation effort that 
have been borne by shareholders.

C. 
Other IS system-related initiatives 
Attachment 8 sets forth the projects underlying Exhibit IV-10 in NorthStar’s report.   
Specifically, each project is described and the capital and operating expense costs are identified. 
Each project contains an explanation for how the costs are allocated and the accounting code 
utilized.  The increase in rent expense over time to the NY Operating Companies is the result of 
the projects in Attachment 8.  These projects are not related to USFP Stabilization, but rather 
reflect work necessary to address a number of systems that need to be replaced or to add needed 
functionality.   
Attachment 8 is comprised of three schedules.  The first is the summary, which provides the 
total depreciation costs being allocated to each of the NY Operating Companies for Calendar 
Years 2012 through 2017.  The subsequent two schedules detail the USFP and Non-USFP 
projects.9 
 
 
 
 
 
 
V. 
Conclusion 
National Grid is confident that USFP Stabilization costs have been borne by shareholders and 
that none of those costs will be recovered from customers.  National Grid is also confident that 
the incremental costs associated with Finance Remediation efforts are being borne shareholders.  
The tracking and review of those costs will continue.  Further, this report demonstrates that the 
increase in annual rent expense to the NY Operating Companies is unrelated to USFP 
Stabilization and is the result of work necessary to address a number of systems that need to be 
replaced or to add needed functionality. 
In accordance with the Implementation Plan, a meeting will be held with Staff to discuss this 
report.   
                                                           
9 For completeness, all projects that are in-service, in-progress and those planned for calendar year 2016 are 
included in Attachment 8.

Appendix 1- Accounting guidance 
 
 
 
 
 
 
1- Background 
On November 5, 2012 (“go-live”), National Grid USA (“NGUSA” or the “Company”) launched the majority of 
system modules and associated business processes necessary to integrate its Human Resources, Supply Chain 
and Finance processes on a single ERP platform (referred to as the US Foundation Project or “USFP”).  The 
remaining modules and business processes were launched in December 2012.  Post go-live, the Company 
experienced significant implementation challenges, which required extensive remedial work to bring the system 
to an acceptable level of performance.  As the implementation challenges were identified, the Company 
responded with significant resources and effort to address the issues.  The goals were twofold – stabilize the 
system and enhance and add new functionality where possible. 
 
By Fall 2014, the Company had stabilized the system and made a number of enhancements.  The purpose of this 
summary is to explain the Company’s conservative approach to capitalizing costs associated with those 
enhancements (as distinguished from expenses associated with stabilizing the system).   
 
 
2- Application of the accounting guidance and booking of costs 
 
2-1 Application of the accounting for pre-go live costs 
 
US GAAP contains specific guidance related to computer software (ASC 350-10).  
 
Per the guidance: 
• 
Capitalization of costs shall begin when both of the following occur:

• 
Preliminary project stage is completed 
• 
Management, with the relevant authority, implicitly or explicitly authorizes and commits to 
funding a computer software project and it is probable that the project will be completed and the 
software will be used to perform the function intended. 
• 
Capitalization of costs shall cease no later than the point at which a computer software project is 
substantially complete and ready for its intended use, that is, after all substantial testing is completed 
(see further details in 2.2 below) 
 
EITF 97-13 (Accounting for Costs Incurred in Connection with a Consulting Contract or an Internal Project 
That Combines Business Process Reengineering and Information Technology Transformation) clarifies the 
accounting treatment for typical components of a business process reengineering/information technology 
transformation project based on whether the item should be capitalized or expense as incurred. 
 
The detailed guidance is presented in Appendix 1. 
 
 
2-2 Application of accounting guidance for post-go live costs 
The accounting literature regarding the capitalization of post post-go live systems costs is subject to 
interpretation and thus subject to disparate application. The Company considered two such interpretations as 
summarized below.  
View A: Capitalization until software is ready for its intended used 
Under this view, costs incurred to develop and implement internal-use software are capitalized until the point at 
which the software is ready for use as intended by management (consistent with “ready for its intended use” 
under ASC 350-40-25-12).  
Under View A, a stated “go-live” date for National Grid’s ERP system is less important than is the point in time 
that the ERP system and its various modules were ready for management’s intended use. 
Thus, View A holds that different modules were ready for use in the condition intended by management at 
different dates, and certain costs incurred to complete those modules beyond 11/5 included true “new build” and 
enhancement activities (most notably in the areas of Payroll and Procure to Pay (P2P)). Additionally, the 
significant amount of costs incurred after 11/5 supports the assertion that the entirety of USFP was not 
completed as of 11/5, and the Company’s SAP ERP system was not fully complete and ready for management’s 
intended use at that date. Since the Company’s ERP system and its various modules were completed and made 
ready according to management’s intended use at differing dates from 11/5 through early 2013, it was 
appropriate to continue capitalizing development costs incurred until the respective individual modules were 
completed. 
View B: Capitalization of enhancements and upgrades 
View B holds the standpoint that costs incurred to develop and implement enhancements and upgrades to 
internal-use software are to be capitalized. That is, in accordance with US GAAP, View B holds that, “In order 
for costs of specified upgrades and enhancements to internal-use computer software to be capitalized in 
accordance with paragraphs 350-40-25-8 through 25-10, it must be probable that those expenditures will result 
in additional functionality” (ASC 350-40-25-7).  
USFP Stabilization and Finance Remediation Report  
 
Case 13-G-0009 - Comprehensive Management and Operations Audit
of National Grid USA's New York Gas Companies 
USFP Capital Expenditure Analysis
Attachment 3 
Summary 
Page 5 of 15
Exhibit ___ (SSP-13) 
Page 28 of 44
219


--- Page 236 ---
In accordance with View B, the Company’s stated “go-live” date for its ERP system establishes the point in time 
at which the internal-use software begins as ready for management’s intended use, and therefore, any additional, 
related costs are to be capitalized only if those costs are probable to realize future economic benefits for the 
Company. Thus, costs incurred only for those activities that add additional functionality to the system, such as to 
generate a new report or to complete and go live with a module that had otherwise been deferred past the 
remaining system’s “go-live” date, and bring benefits that enhance the current state of the existing ERP system 
are to be capitalized.  
View B more strictly interprets the Company’s stated “go-live” date of 11/5 as the point at which the system 
development phase has ended, and after which incremental costs can only be capitalized if they are 
enhancements to the original system.  That is, costs incurred after the go-live date are assumed to be for ongoing 
maintenance activities, “bug fixes” or other remediation activities and thus expensed as incurred. However, 
costs which can be clearly demonstrated to result in enhancements and upgrades to the system, then such costs 
may be capitalized. 
Comparison of results of applying View A and View B, and conclusion 
View A and View B differ in the principle core to the capitalization of various costs related to the Company’s 
ERP system: the point in time at which capitalization is to end.  
In an effort to ensure that customers do not bear costs associated with the implementation issues and stabilizing 
the system, the Company selected View B - the most conservative approach. Accordingly, after go-live, only 
costs incurred related to the development of new features, functionality or modules in the SAP program that did 
not exist at the time the software went live have been capitalized.  
2-3 Accounting for post-go live costs 
 
The accounting treatment is as follows: 
• 
All capital spends are recorded at NG USA Service Company 
• 
Associated rental charges are allocated to operating companies per the bill pool generally applicable to 
each company  
• 
All Operation &Maintenance costs are recorded at NG USA (“Parent”) level to ensure that those costs 
are not passed through to the customers.  
 
 
3- Review process of pre Go-live capitalizable costs 
 
The Company performed a complete review of all pre go-live capitalized costs to ensure that they meet the 
capitalization criteria as per the technical accounting guidance. 
 
The company performed the following tasks during the review process: 
1- Review appropriateness of expenses capitalized: 
• 
Obtain all capitalized costs by expense type 
• 
Ensure the nature of those expenses is eligible for capitalization per the technical accounting guidance. 
 
During this review, the company identified that certain training costs for $23.8M and data conversion costs 
for $1.3M were improperly capitalized. Those costs were reclassified to operating expenses in the books of 
the parent company.

2- Ensure each costs is supported by appropriate documentation (e.g vendors costs shall be supported by 
invoices). 
 
See detailed support for pre-go live costs in Attachment III (Section III, B-1). 
 
 
4- Process for the identification of capitalizable Post-Go live costs  
 
The company followed the steps below to determine the capitalizable costs incurred post go-live: 
 
Step 1- Identify the scope of projects in USFP by obtaining from the Decision Support Group the list of 
sanctioned projects under the program. 
 
Step 2- Determine the scope of projects that include capitalizable activities. 
For each sanctioned project, obtain charters, which provide a high level description of the objective of each 
project.  From the description, perform a pre-determination of the appropriate accounting treatment (per the 
approach described in 2.2 above).  The analysis is then corroborated through the following activities: 
• 
Conduct interviews with project leads 
• 
Leverage the RICEFW (Reports, Interfaces, Conversions, Extensions, Forms and Workflows) to 
identify new builds in the system.  The RICEFW tracks all development objects by project and is 
approved by the USFP delivery lead.  Each object is classified as either “new” or “change” with “new” 
meaning that a new build is added in the system.  As such, new development objects indicate that the 
project includes capitalizable activities (added functionalities).  
 
Step 3- Determine the costs capitalizable for each project identified in step 2: 
• 
Obtain the list of vendors by project from the project leads. 
• 
Obtain the costs of each project by vendor.  In general, invoices detail costs by project identification 
number.  If the project is not specifically identified on the invoice, vendors are requested to provide this  
detail for the purpose of the analysis. 
• 
Determine the pool of costs that are eligible for capitalization.  The main phases of each project are 
Plan, Design, Build, Test, Go-live and Post go-live support.  Planning activities and Post go-live support 
are not capitalizable activities and are deducted from the pool of eligible costs. 
• 
Furthermore, each project might include activities for new builds and changes to the system, 
Consequently, a capitalization percentage to be applied to each pool of eligible costs is calculated.  The 
percentage is determined based on the ratio new versus change in the RICEFW as well as any other 
relevant information obtained from the vendors and project leads. 
• 
Percentages are applied to the pool of eligible costs for each project. 
 
Step 4- Ensure reasonableness of costs to be capitalized by comparing capitalized costs calculated by project 
with budgeted costs.  Discrepancies deemed significant (as assessed by the Decision Support Group) are 
investigated and explained. 
 
Step 5- Management reviews the costs proposed for capitalization and ensures that the amount seems 
appropriate and reasonable. 
 
Step 6- Capitalizable costs are recorded in the books of the Service Company. 
 
See detail support for post-go live costs in Attachment III (Section III, B-2).

Conclusion 
The Company believes that its conservative reading of the accounting guidance, as well as the strict process it 
followed for the identification and review of capitalizable costs ensures that costs have been accounted for 
properly.   The Company is confident that stabilization costs have been expensed and only those costs associated 
with new functionality and enhancements have been capitalized.

accounting treatment for typical components of a business process reengineering/information technology 
transformation project based on whether the item should be:  
a) Capitalized in accordance with ASC350-40 internal-use software 
b) Expensed as incurred in accordance with the task force consensus and ASC350-40 internal-use

Additionally, costs incurred related to the development of significant new features, functionality or modules in 
the SAP program that did not exist at the time the software shall be capitalized. In particular, upgrades and 
enhancements are defined as modifications to existing internal-use software that result in additional 
functionality—that is, modifications to enable the software to perform tasks that it was previously incapable of 
performing. Upgrades and enhancements normally require new software specifications and may also require a 
change to all or part of the existing software specifications (ASC350-40-05). 
 
To the extent that costs relate to post-go-live support, training, maintenance, minor bug fixes, and servicing of 
the software, they should be expensed as incurred, as they do not meet the criteria for costs incurred in the 
development phase, and do not significantly contribute to additional functionality of the software according to 
ASC 350.  
 
 
US GAAP contains specific guidance related to computer software that consists of more than one component or 
module:  “For example, an entity may develop an accounting software system containing three elements: a 
general ledger, an accounts payable subledger, and an accounts receivable subledger. In this example, each 
element might be viewed as a component or module of the entire accounting software system.  The guidance 
shall be applied to individual components or modules.”  (ASC 350-40-15-2.d.)

ASC 350-40 does not define “maintenance”.  However, maintenance is defined as follows in ASC 985-20-20: 
“Activities undertaken after the product is available for general release to customers to correct errors or keep the 
product updated with current information. Those activities include routine changes and additions.” 
Maintenance releases are usually distributed on an ad hoc, as needed, as available, basis or, if scheduled, often 
occur at about six-month intervals. 
 
 
Entities that cannot separate internal costs on a reasonably cost-effective basis between maintenance and 
relatively minor upgrades and enhancements shall expense such costs as incurred (ASC350-40-25).

See below USGAAP guidance applicable for the capitalization of costs related to the implementation of the ERP.
US GAAP
AUTHORITATIVE ACCOUNTING GUIDANCE
US GAAP contains specific guidance related to computer software that consists of more than one component or 
module:  “For example, an entity may develop an accounting software system containing three elements: a 
general ledger, an accounts payable subledger, and an accounts receivable subledger. In this example, each
element might be viewed as a component or module of the entire accounting software system.  The guidance
shall be applied to individual components or modules.”  (ASC 350-40-15-2.d.)  
 
As such, the accounting treatment of post go-live costs shall be determined for each module individually. 
 
Per guidance in ASC 350-10-25-12 and 14: 
• 
Capitalization of costs shall begin when both of the following occur: 
• 
Preliminary project stage is completed 
• 
Management, with the relevant authority, implicitly or explicitly authorizes and commits to 
funding a computer software project and it is probable that the project will be completed and the
software will be used to perform the function intended 
Capitalization of costs shall cease no later than the point at which a computer software project is substantially 
complete and ready for its intended use, that is, after all substantial testing is completed. 
Additionally, costs incurred related to the development of significant new features, functionality or modules in
the SAP program that did not exist at the time the software shall be capitalized. In particular, upgrades and 
enhancements are defined as modifications to existing internal-use software that result in additional 
functionality—that is, modifications to enable the software to perform tasks that it was previously incapable of 
performing. Upgrades and enhancements normally require new software specifications and may also require a
change to all or part of the existing software specifications (ASC350-40-05). 
 
To the extent that costs relate to post-go-live support, training, maintenance, minor bug fixes, and servicing of 
the software, they should be expensed as incurred, as they do not meet the criteria for costs incurred in the
development phase, and do not significantly contribute to additional functionality of the software according to
ASC 350.  
 
ASC 350-40 does not define “maintenance”. However, maintenance is defined as follows in ASC 985-20-20:
“Activities undertaken after the product is available for general release to customers to correct errors or keep the 
product updated with current information. Those activities include routine changes and additions.” 
Maintenance releases are usually distributed on an ad hoc, as needed, as available, basis or, if scheduled, often 
occur at about six-month intervals. 
 
ASC 350-40 does not define “enhancement”, either. However, enhancement is defined as follows in ASC 985-
20-20: “Improvements to an existing product that are intended to extend the life […] of the original product. 
Enhancements normally require a product design and may require a redesign of all or part of the existing
product.” 
 
Entities that cannot separate internal costs on a reasonably cost-effective basis between maintenance and 
relatively minor upgrades and enhancements shall expense such costs as incurred (ASC350-40-25).

Enhancement Release 1 and FERC on HANA projects and their benefits

Page 1 of 3 
 
Enhancements or Upgrades to SAP – October 2015 Through December 2016 
 
EHR1 – PMO (In Service December 2015) 
This project was comprised of various enhancements including improvements to performance 
reporting.  Also, this project implemented an upgrade to the most current version of the SAP 
solution to support long term solution stability and to improve the ease with which employees 
can use a number of SAP functions including time entry, time approval, travel and expense 
approval and commonly used finance transactions.  The costs associated with this project include 
those required to manage the portfolio of EHR1 projects and provide the necessary analytics.  
The costs are allocated to All Companies. 
 
EHR1 - Supply Chain (In Service December 2015) 
This project was comprised of various enhancements to improve supply chain processes.  For 
example, improvements to the inventory and warehouse management processes to improve 
inventory accuracy, standardize warehousing operations and increase visibility to warehouse 
delivery performance.  The project improved usability of the Supplier Relationship Management 
(“SRM”) system to update the contract amount based on limit confirmation and purchase order 
closure, support standard functionalities like pricing scales and service master, and reduce the 
required follow-ups and manual workarounds in the current service procurement process.  In 
addition, this project included phasing out utilization of an outdated information technology 
(“IT”) network for use by a limited number of suppliers that will not be supported by replacing it 
with a new network capable of supporting any number of suppliers.  This project also included 
enhancements to Fleet management processes including automated fleet rental data, better 
reporting for  annual mileage for DOT vehicles, fleet work order by task, NYC consumption, 
garage vendor spending, all of which reduces the manual labor necessary to manage fleet data, 
improve fleet inventory accuracy, and improves procurement processes.  The costs are allocated 
to All Companies. 
 
EHR1 – Finance (In Service December 2015) 
This project was comprised of various enhancements to improve finance processes.  For 
example, improvements to the cross company purchasing solution (“CCPS”) process enable 
accounting entries at the time of service confirmation for service and limit purchase orders, 
which improves data accuracy and reduces manual efforts.  Fleet Operations was using a basic 
design in SAP that was enhanced to improve recording of end-to-end information.  
237


--- Page 255 ---
Exhibit __ (SSP-14) 
Page 2 of 3 
 
Enhancements were performed for the Fleet end-to-end financial and inventory processes.  These 
enhancements improve the Procure to Pay and Record to Report processes, simplify cost flow, 
improve accuracy of reporting of vehicle utilization and reduce time to process settlements.  This 
project will also automated certain purchase order and invoice processing.  The costs are 
allocated to All Companies. 
 
EHR1 - IT Delivery (In Service December 2015) 
This included software licenses and consulting costs in support of EHR1 projects. These costs 
will be directly charged to the EHR1 projects when actual costs are finalized in January 2016. 
 
EHR1 – Payroll (In Service December 2015) 
This project was comprised of various enhancements to deliver significant improvements to 
payroll processes.  For example, it aligned the step progression process across National Grid, 
thereby mitigating risk of payroll errors and facilitating compliance with collective bargaining 
agreements.  This project also included SAP enhancements to automate the human resource 
benefits eligibility enrollment process, which minimizes the manual effort to enroll employees in 
pension, 401K, and supplemental insurance benefits.  These enhancements will avoid delays in 
enrollment and reduce retroactive adjustments.  Further, enhancements were completed to 
automate certain payroll processes to improve run times, which will allow employees 
incremental time to enter their hours worked.  This will facilitate accurate time entry and 
improve data accuracy.  The costs are allocated to All Companies. 
 
EHR1 – BPS (In Service December 2015) 
This project included enhancements to improve various business processes.  For example, the 
process for providing or rescinding user access to SAP was manual, which increased the 
likelihood of error.  This project automated the process and builds a more sustainable and mature 
controls environment by improving the quality of key controls.  In addition, the project will 
automate the process that allows  business owners to accurately handle business document errors 
for the fiscal year in a timely manner as well as provide monthly aged reports to National Grid 
management to help assess business data accuracy by area.  This project also enhances the 
capabilities of the current SAP development environment to better manage system maintenance 
and the change management process.  This project implemented a tool to manage change 
requests for SAP and PowerPlan.  The costs are allocated to All Companies.  
238


--- Page 256 ---
Exhibit __ (SSP-14) 
Page 3 of 3 
 
 
USFP - EHR1 - S005242 M112 Systemic Improvement (“FERC on HANA”) (In Service March 
2016) 
This project will decommission the existing FERC module within the SAP ECC system and 
relocate it to a new HANA cloud-based architecture, allowing for real-time FERC reporting 
abilities.  FERC data will be created in parallel with all other financial data.  Real-time 
processing and reporting during the close process allows for correction of issues, to the extent 
they are necessary, sooner.  Entries can be made directly to the FERC accounts allowing for 
traceability to original documents. The costs are allocated to All Companies.

USFP Enhancement Projects included in the Rate Year and Data Years

Enhancements / Upgrades to SAP – Rate and Data Years 
 
Access Violation (& Regulation) Management  (In Service April 2017) - This project will 
enable automated access control and monitoring to address Sarbanes-Oxley and other regulatory 
requirements, as well as IT system controls.  
US SAP: Business Planning Consolidation – HANA (In Service April 2017) -The Business 
Planning and Consolidation (BPC) application, supporting National Grid’s monthly financial 
close and reporting, will be upgraded.  The upgrade will provide increased accessibility to 
planning capabilities in the system and provide improved forecasting capabilities.  In addition, it 
will enable accelerated real time insights to variances for improved decision making. 
US SAP: Improve Performance Reporting (In Service December 2018) - This project will 
apply process and technology solutions to enable the recording, extraction and reporting of unit 
cost productivity and key performance indicators supporting business performance excellence 
goals and the increasing need for real-time and detailed data to support planning and 
management decisions . 
PII Project - Security & Controls (In Service April 2019) - Currently, to protect personally 
identifiable information for individuals within the SAP application, National Grid spends 
significant time and manual effort to apply data masking techniques within the development, 
quality assurance and project environments.  This project will provide the capability to automate 
the protection of sensitive fields and personal private information while reducing the risk of error 
through manual processing. 
Blanket Work Order Pilot (In Service April 2019) - This project implements blanket work 
orders and common industry practices of single work orders and consolidated process 
accounting.  The project also simplifies and optimizes the work order lifecycle processes, which 
improves data accuracy, reduces administrative burdens and facilitates capitalizing work orders 
in a timely manner.  
PowerPlan Module Enhancements (In Service April 2019) - This project delivers 
improvements to data quality and reduces the cycle time for the monthly and fiscal year-end 
financial close process.  New software modules will be deployed to support such capabilities as 
improved data capture from mobile devises, improved data management, and enhanced tax and 
asset management functionality.
